\displaystyle m::=\hskip 8.50012pt

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}{\;\overline{v}}\rangle

Traces

\displaystyle\alpha::=\hskip 8.50012pt

\displaystyle[\,]~|~m\;{::}\;\alpha~|~\alpha{\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}\roundcap\put(0.1,0.5){\line{1}{0}{0.8}}\put(0.35,0.1){\line{0}{1}{0.8}}\put(0.65,0.1){\line{0}{1}{0.8}}\end{picture}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}\roundcap\put(0.1,0.5){\line{1}{0}{0.8}}\put(0.35,0.1){\line{0}{1}{0.8}}\put(0.65,0.1){\line{0}{1}{0.8}}\end{picture}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}\roundcap\put(0.1,0.5){\line{1}{0}{0.8}}\put(0.35,0.1){\line{0}{1}{0.8}}\put(0.65,0.1){\line{0}{1}{0.8}}\end{picture}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}\roundcap\put(0.1,0.5){\line{1}{0}{0.8}}\put(0.35,0.1){\line{0}{1}{0.8}}\put(0.65,0.1){\line{0}{1}{0.8}}\end{picture}}}}}}\alpha

Notations

\displaystyle e_{1};e_{2}\doteq\hskip 8.50012pt

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{let}}}}}\ {\_}\ {=}\ {e_{1}}\ {\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{in}}}}}\ {e_{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip-71.13188pt{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{let}}}}}\ {x{:}t}\ {=}\ {{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{assume}}}}}\ {\phi(x)}}\ {\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{in}}}}}\ {e}\doteq\hskip 8.50012pt

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{let}}}}}\ {x{:}t}\ {=}\ {{\tt rand\_int()}}\ {\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{in}}}}}\ {{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{assume}}}}}\ {\phi(x)};e}

Figure 2
.
Syntax of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{C}
expressions

We formalize our approach using a core language,
\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{C}
, for
expressing
trace generators
– nondeterministic programs that
probe the behaviors of a black-box system under test via a set of
(effectful) operators that the system provides. This calculus is
equipped with a type system that characterizes the set of traces that
a program
must
be able to produce,
if
the system under
test produces appropriate responses. The syntax of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{C}
is shown in

Fig. 2
; the calculus includes both pure and
effectful operations (
\mathit{op}
and
\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}
), nondeterministic
choices (
\oplus
), recursion (
{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{fix}}}}}
), and
{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{assume}}}}}
and

{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{assert}}}}}
statements in the style of solver-aided
languages
(
Bodik et al. 2010
)
. As we saw in
Sec. 2
,
asynchronous effects can be modeled as a pair of
\boldsymbol{{\tt opReq}}
/

\boldsymbol{{\tt opResp}}
operations, where the former generates an id (given as

\iota
in the syntax) that can then be subsequently referenced when
performing a corresponding response operation.

\displaystyle\text{{Operator Semantics }}\ \framebox{$\vDash\mathit{op}(\overline{c})\Downarrow c\hskip 8.50012pt\alpha\vDash\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}(\overline{c})\Downarrow c$}

\displaystyle\text{{Operational Semantics }}\ \framebox{${\alpha}\vDash{e}{\xhookrightarrow{\alpha}\,}{e}$}

StPOp

\vDash\mathit{op}(\overline{c})\Downarrow c^{\prime}
{\alpha}\vDash{\mathit{op}\;\overline{c}\;}{\xhookrightarrow{[\;]}\,}{c^{\prime}}

StEfOp

\alpha\vDash\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}\overline{c}\Downarrow c^{\prime}
{\alpha}\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}\;\overline{c}\;}{\xhookrightarrow{[\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}\;\overline{c}\;c^{\prime}]}\,}{c^{\prime}}

Figure 3
.
Selected reduction rules

\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{C}
is equipped with a small-step operational semantics similar to
other calculi for trace-based type systems
(
Zhou et al. 2024
)
; selected
reduction rules are shown in

Fig. 3
.
5
5

5

The full reduction rules; complete definitions of the auxiliary typing
relations (including well-formedness); the full typing rules beyond

Fig. 5
; further denotational details; and
proofs of the theorems in
Sec. 3.3
are all given in the
supplemental material.

The reduction relation

{\alpha}\vDash{e}{\xhookrightarrow{~\alpha^{\prime}}\,}{e^{\prime}}
constrains the output trace

\alpha^{\prime}
produced by executing
e
based on a
history trace

\alpha
. The evaluation of pure operations (rule
StPOp
) makes no use of trace, either history or output. The
result of an effectful operation, in contrast, can depend on the
events that preceded it, and it produces an output trace containing an
event whose payload contains both the arguments and return value of
the operation.

3.1.
Type System

The syntax of types in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{C}
is shown in
Fig. 4
.
Types include
pure
refinement types, which describe pure
computations, and underapproximate Hoare Automata Types (
u
Hat
s),
which describe effectful computations.
Pure refinement types are similar to those found in prior
underapproximate refinement type systems
(
Zhou et al. 2023
)
, and
allow base types (e.g.,
int
) to be further constrained by a
logical formula or qualifier. In contrast to traditional type systems
where a pure type qualifier
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{{\nu}{:}{b}\,}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}^{o}}
constrains every value a
pure expression
may
evaluate to; the type qualifier

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{}{{\nu}{:}{b}\,|\,{\phi}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}^{u}}{}
constrains a subset of the values it
must

evaluate to. For example, the constant value
1
can be assigned the
types
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{{\nu}{:}{{\tt int}}\,}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}^{o}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{{\nu}{:}{{\tt int}}\,}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}^{o}}
– the value 1
satisfies the constraint in both
overapproximate
qualifiers –
but, 1 cannot be assigned the type,

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{}{{\nu}{:}{{\tt int}}\,|\,{\nu=1\lor\nu=2}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}^{u}}{}
, since it cannot evaluate to both
1 and 2 as required by this
underapproximate
qualifier. The
term
1\oplus 2\oplus 3
, in contrast, does have this
underapproximate type.

Underapproximate Hoare Automata Types

Similar to other recent trace-based type systems
(
Zhou et al. 2024
)
,
\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{C}

uses
Symbolic Finite Automata
(SFAs)
(
Veanes 2013
;
D’Antoni and Veanes 2014
;
D’Antoni and Veanes 2017
)
to qualify the traces generated
by effectful computations; in contrast to those prior works, the

u
Hat
s in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{C}{}
use SFAs to
underapproximate
the set of effects
that a program must produce. A
u
Hat

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{H}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,x{:}t\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{F}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}
consists
of a pure refinement type
t
and two SFAs: a
history
SFA
H

that constrains the history trace under which a term is executed, and a

future
SFA
F
that describes a subset of the events that it
must generate as a result.
u
Hat
s represent SFAs using symbolic
regular expressions (SREs), but in principle could support any
encoding of SFAs, e.g., Symbolic LTL
f

(
De Giacomo and Vardi 2013
)
. SREs support

symbolic events
,
{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}\;{\overline{x}}~|~{\phi}}{\rangle}
, atomic
predicates that describe an effectful operation
\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}
whose
arguments
\overline{x}
satisfy the qualifier
\phi
. SREs provide a
convenient language for writing SFAs, supporting versions of the
standard regex operators shown in
Fig. 4
, including
union
A\lor A
, sequence
A{\cdot}A
, and Kleene star
A^{*}

operators.

Symbolic Regex

\displaystyle H,F,A::=\hskip 9.24994pt

\displaystyle\emptyset~|~\epsilon~|~{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}\;{\overline{x}}~|~{\phi}}{\rangle}~|~A\lor A~|~A{\cdot}A~|~A^{*}~|~A\setminus A~|~A\land A~|~\bullet

Pure Refinement Types

\displaystyle t::=\hskip 9.24994pt

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{}{{\nu}{:}{b}\,|\,{\phi}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}^{u}}{}~|~x{:}t{\,\shortrightarrow\,}t~|~x{:}t{\,\shortrightarrow\,}\tau~|~x{:}b{\,\dashrightarrow\,}t~|~\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}op}}}{:}s{\,\shortrightarrow\,}t

Underapproximate HATs

\displaystyle\tau::=\hskip 9.24994pt

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{H}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,x{:}t\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{F}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}~|~\tau\,\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\sqcap}}\,\tau

Type Contexts

\displaystyle\Gamma::=\hskip 9.24994pt

\displaystyle\emptyset~|~x{:}t,\Gamma

Type Aliases

\displaystyle\;\;{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}~|~{\phi}}{\rangle}\doteq{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}\;{\overline{x}}~|~{\phi}}{\rangle}\;\;{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}\;{\overline{v}}}{\rangle}\doteq{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}\;{\overline{x}}~|~{\overline{x=v}}}{\rangle}\;\;{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}}{\rangle}\doteq{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}\;{\overline{x}}~|~{\top}}{\rangle}\;\;A_{1}A_{2}\doteq A_{1}{\cdot}A_{2}\;\;b\doteq{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{}{{\nu}{:}{b}\,|\,{\top}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}^{u}}{}

Figure 4
.
\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{C}
types

The figure also gives the definition of the type aliases that

Sec. 2
used to limit user annotation burden. When the
fields of a symbolic event are clear from context, we omit its
arguments
\overline{x}
, e.g.,
\langle\boldsymbol{{\tt push}}\;|\;v>0\rangle

means
\langle\boldsymbol{{\tt push}}\;v\;|\;v>0\rangle
. We omit quantifiers
with equality constraints, e.g., writing

\langle\boldsymbol{{\tt push}}\;v\;|\;v=3\rangle
, as
{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt push}}\;{3}}{\rangle}
, and the
top (
\top
) qualifier in symbolic events and types.

Example 3.1 (Ghost Events).

Consider an effectful stack library implemented as a fixed-size
array whose elements grow monotonically. An incorrect implementation
that fails to grow the array when the stack is full may cause

\boldsymbol{{\tt push}}
operations to be dropped. To detect this violation, we
need to explore traces of the following form, where the last pop
operation raises an “empty stack” exception:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{{\tt push}}({1});\boldsymbol{{\tt push}}({2});\boldsymbol{{\tt push}}({3});\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}({2});\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}({1});{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}({?})}

Because we would like the
u
Hat
specifications for
\boldsymbol{{\tt push}}

and
\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}
to guide the synthesis of a test generator, they
should be interpreted as underapproximations, consisting of nested
pairs of
\boldsymbol{{\tt push}}
and
\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}
events. This can be expressed
by the following
u
Hat
specifications, using ghost events

\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}pushI}}}
and
\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}popI}}}
as shown below:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{{\tt push}}:~n{:}{\tt int}{\,\dashrightarrow\,}y{:}{\tt int}{\,\dashrightarrow\,}x{:}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{}{{\nu}{:}{{\tt int}}\,|\,{y<\nu}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}^{u}}{}{\,\shortrightarrow\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{{\tt Last}({\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt pushI}}}\;{n\;y}}{\rangle})}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,{\tt unit}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt push}}\;{x}}{\rangle}{\cdot}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt pushI}}}\;{n{+}1\;x}}{\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}:~n{:}{\tt int}{\,\dashrightarrow\,}m{:}{\tt int}{\,\dashrightarrow\,}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{{\tt Last}({\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt popI}}}\;{m\;\_}}{\rangle})\land{\tt Last}({\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt pushI}}}\;{n\;\_}}{\rangle})\land({\bullet^{*}}{\cdot}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt pushI}}}\;{(n{-}m)\;x}}{\rangle}{\cdot}{\bullet^{*}})}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,x{:}{\tt int}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}\;{x}}{\rangle}{\cdot}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt popI}}}\;{m{+}1\;x}}{\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}

Intuitively, the
\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}pushI}}}
and
\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}popI}}}
ghost events are
simply
\boldsymbol{{\tt push}}
and
\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}
events equipped with indices that
record the number of push and pop operations. As seen here, ghost events allow
u
Hat

specifications to capture latent data dependencies between effectful
operations. The signature for
\boldsymbol{{\tt push}}
requires
that the new pushed value (
x
) is greater than the last pushed value
(
y
), and adds a new ghost event
\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}pushI}}}
with an
incremented index (i.e.,
{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt pushI}}}\;{n{+}1\;x}}{\rangle}
).
The
signature for
\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}
also records the number of pop operations
in the same way (i.e.,
{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt popI}}}\;{m{+}1\;x}}{\rangle}
); additionally, it
requires the last popped value (
x
) to be equal to the

(n{-}m)^{th}
pushed value, reflecting the “last-in-first-out”
property of the stack.

3.2.
Typing Rules

\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{C}{}
is equipped with a pair of typing judgments on pure and
effectful expressions,
\Gamma\vdash v:t
and
\Gamma\vdash e:{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{H}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,x{:}t\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{F}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}
, respectively. Both judgments rely on a type
context,
\Gamma
, that maps from variables to pure refinement types
(i.e.,
t
). As in prior work
(
Zhou et al. 2023
)
, typing contexts are not
allowed to contain
u
Hat
s— doing so breaks several structural
properties (e.g., weakening) that are used to prove type safety. Both
rules are parameterized over an additional context
\Delta
, which
provides types for pure and effectful operators.

Auxiliary typing relations

Our type system also relies on three additional auxiliary
relations. The first is a well-formedness relation
\Gamma\vdash^{\textbf{WF}}\tau
that ensures, among other things, that all
qualifiers appearing in a type
\tau
are closed under the current
typing context
\Gamma
and that the verification conditions generated by
the synthesis algorithm presented in the next section can be encoded
as effectively propositional (EPR) sentences
(
Ramsey 1987
)
, which
can be efficiently handled by an off-the-shelf theorem prover such as
Z3
(
de Moura and Bjørner 2008
)
. We also require the
history regex
H
to not be empty, which guarantees that it represents
a meaningful underapproximate precondition.

Auxiliary Typing

\displaystyle\framebox{$\Gamma\vdash^{\textbf{WF}}\tau\hskip 8.50012pt\Gamma\vdash A\subseteq A\hskip 8.50012pt\Gamma\vdash\tau<:\tau$}

Typing

\displaystyle\framebox{$\Gamma\vdash\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}:t\hskip 8.50012pt\Gamma\vdash v:t\hskip 8.50012pt\Gamma\vdash e:\tau$}

SubG

\Gamma\vdash^{\textbf{WF}}\tau[x\mapsto v]

\Gamma\vdash x{:}b{\,\dashrightarrow\,}\tau<:\tau[x\mapsto v]

SubHF

\Gamma\vdash t_{1}<:t_{2}

\Gamma,x{:}t_{1}\vdash F_{2}\subseteq F_{1}

\Gamma,x{:}t_{1}\vdash H_{1}\subseteq H_{2}

\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{H_{1}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,x{:}t_{1}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{F_{1}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}<:{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{H_{2}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,x{:}t_{2}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{F_{2}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}

TOpHis

\Gamma\vdash\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}:\overline{x{:}t_{x}}{\,\shortrightarrow\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{H}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,t\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{F}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}

\Gamma,\overline{x{:}t_{x}}\vdash H^{\prime}\subseteq H

\Gamma,\overline{x{:}t_{x}}\not\vdash H^{\prime}\subseteq\emptyset

\Gamma\vdash\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}:\overline{x{:}t_{x}}{\,\shortrightarrow\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{H^{\prime}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,t\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{F}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}

TRet

\Gamma\vdash v:t

\Gamma\vdash v:{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{H}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,t\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}

TOpCtx

\Delta(\boldsymbol{{\tt op}})=t

\Gamma\vdash\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}:t

TEffOp

\Gamma\vdash\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}:\overline{x_{i}{:}t_{i}}{\,\shortrightarrow\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{H}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,t\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}\;{\overline{x_{i}}}}{\rangle}{\cdot}F}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}

\forall i.\;\Gamma\vdash v_{i}:t_{i}

\Gamma\vdash\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}{\;\overline{v_{i}}}:{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{H}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,t\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}\;{\overline{v_{i}}}}{\rangle}{\cdot}F}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}

TChoice

\Gamma\vdash e_{1}:\tau_{1}

\Gamma\vdash e_{2}:\tau_{2}

\Gamma\vdash e_{1}\oplus e_{2}:\tau_{1}\,\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\sqcap}}\,\tau_{2}

TLet

\Gamma\vdash e_{1}:{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{H}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,x{:}t_{x}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{F_{1}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}

\Gamma,x{:}t_{x}\vdash e_{2}:{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{H{\cdot}F_{1}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,t\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{F_{2}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}

\Gamma\vdash{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{let}}}}}\ {x}\ {=}\ {e_{1}}\ {\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{in}}}}}\ {e_{2}}:{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{H}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,t\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{F_{1}{\cdot}F_{2}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}

TFun

\Gamma,x{:}t_{x}\vdash e:\tau

\Gamma\vdash\lambda{x}.{e}:x{:}t_{x}{\,\shortrightarrow\,}\tau

TFixBase

\Gamma\vdash{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{fix}}}}}{f}.\lambda{x}.{e}:\overline{x{:}s}{\,\shortrightarrow\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{H}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{}{{\nu}{:}{b}\,|\,{\bot}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}^{u}}{}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{\emptyset}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}

TFixInd

\Gamma\vdash{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{fix}}}}}{f}.\lambda{x}.{e}:t

\Gamma,f{:}t\vdash\lambda{x}.{e}:t^{\prime}

\Gamma\vdash{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{fix}}}}}{f}.\lambda{x}.{e}:t^{\prime}

Figure 5
.
Selected typing rules.

The type system also uses a semantic subtyping relation tailored for
underapproximation;
Fig. 5
presents the
key subtyping rule for
u
Hat
s. The rule
SubG
allows
the instantiation of a ghost variable in a
u
Hat
into
well-formed types. The rule
SubHF
uses an auxiliary
inclusion relation on SREs
\Gamma\vdash A_{1}\subseteq A_{2}
to relate
the history and future regexes of two
u
Hat
s under the current
type context
\Gamma
, as well as the return type using the standard
subtyping relation on pure refinement types. Notably, because our
typing judgements are underapproximate, this subtyping relation is
covariant in the history regex and contravariant in the future
regex, yielding behavior similar to the reverse rule of consequence
found in Incorrectness Logic (IL)
(
O’Hearn 2019
)
. Like that rule,

SubHF
allows us to strengthen a postcondition (i.e., shrink
the future trace), and weaken the precondition (i.e., enlarge the
history trace). Since the future automata in a
u
Hat
can refer
to the pure value it returns, the inclusion check between the future
automata extends the typing context with a binding for the return
value.

A subset of our typing rules is shown in

Fig. 5
. All our
rules assume any types they use are well-formed, so we elide the
corresponding well-formedness judgments from their premises. The

TRet
rule requires the future regex of a pure term
(
\epsilon
) to only accept the empty trace. The operator type is
retrieved from the global operator context
\Delta
using the

TOpCtx
rule. For a given event, there may be multiple
reachable history traces (underapproximate preconditions) that can be
allowed by an operator that is determined by the context in which the
operator executes, and by the operator type alone. We, therefore,
expect that the operator type found in the operator context is the
most precise one, i.e., it doesn’t allow any unreachable history
traces, and admits underapproximation specialization. The rule

TOpHis
can then freely choose a
non-empty
subset of the reachable
history traces to align with the actual calling context. The
application rule
TEffOp
requires the arguments of an
effectful operator to be well-typed against its parameter types; it
records the effect as an event in the future trace along with any
additional ghost events (
F
). The nondeterministic choice operator
is typed using the
TChoice
rule which merges the
u
Hat
s
of the two branches. The
TLet
rule requires the binding
expression
e_{1}
to generate the first part of its future trace
F_{1}
,
with the rest of trace produced by the body expression
e_{2}
under a
new history that includes
F_{1}
. The typing rule for functions
(
TFun
) is standard, while the rule for recursive functions is
modeled after the corresponding IL rule, unrolling the recursion as
needed. The
FixBase
rule types any recursive function with a

u
Hat
lacking reachability constraints (i.e.,

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{H}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{}{{\nu}{:}{b}\,|\,{\bot}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}^{u}}{}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{\emptyset}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}
). In contrast, the

FixInd
rule permits typing a recursive function with type

t^{\prime}
by requiring it to have another type
t
, and by checking that
its body can be assigned type
t^{\prime}
under the assumption that the
function itself has type
t
.

Example 3.2 (Operator Application Typing).

We present a typing derivation for a simple generator that generates

\boldsymbol{{\tt push}}
and
\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}
operations using the signatures from
Example
3.1
. Consider how we might type check the
following expression:

\displaystyle\emptyset\vdash{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{let}}}}}\ {x{:}{\tt int}}\ {=}\ {{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{assume}}}}}\ {0<x}}\ {\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{in}}}}}\ {\boldsymbol{{\tt push}}\;x;\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}}:

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt pushI}}}\;{0\;0}}{\rangle}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt popI}}}\;{0\;0}}{\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,x{:}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{}{{\nu}{:}{{\tt int}}\,|\,{0<\nu}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}^{u}}{}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt push}}\;{x}}{\rangle}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt pushI}}}\;{1\;x}}{\rangle}{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}\;{x}}{\rangle}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt popI}}}\;{1\;x}}{\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}

The signature of the generator is a
u
Hat
that assumes a history
trace in which the stack is empty (i.e., the ghost events

\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}pushI}}}
and
\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}popI}}}
both hold a
0
index and a
dummy value). The body of the generator generates a positive number

x
using
{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{assume}}}}}
; the underapproximate refinement type of

x
signifies that it must evaluate to every positive integer. The
generator then is expected to pop the same value. We can thus type
check its two effectful operation applications using the following
judgement:

\displaystyle x{:}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{}{{\nu}{:}{{\tt int}}\,|\,{0<\nu}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}^{u}}{}\vdash\boldsymbol{{\tt push}}\;x;\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}:

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\ {\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt pushI}}}\;{0\;0}}{\rangle}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt popI}}}\;{0\;0}}{\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,x{:}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{}{{\nu}{:}{{\tt int}}\,|\,{0<\nu}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}^{u}}{}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt push}}\;{x}}{\rangle}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt pushI}}}\;{1\;x}}{\rangle}{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}\;{x}}{\rangle}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt popI}}}\;{1\;x}}{\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}

Using the
TEffOp
rule to type the first
\boldsymbol{{\tt push}}

effect, we retrieve the operator’s types from the operator context

\Delta
:

\displaystyle...\vdash\boldsymbol{{\tt push}}:n{:}{\tt int}{\,\dashrightarrow\,}y{:}{\tt int}{\,\dashrightarrow\,}

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\ \ x{:}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{}{{\nu}{:}{{\tt int}}\,|\,{y<\nu}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}^{u}}{}{\,\shortrightarrow\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{{\tt Last}({\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt pushI}}}\;{n\;y}}{\rangle})}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,{\tt unit}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt push}}\;{x}}{\rangle}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt pushI}}}\;{n{+}1\;x}}{\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}

(by
TOpCtx
)

We can then apply the subtyping rule
(
SubG
) to instantiate the signature’s ghost variables
(e.g.,
n\mapsto 0
and
y\mapsto 0
):

\displaystyle...\vdash\boldsymbol{{\tt push}}:x{:}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{}{{\nu}{:}{{\tt int}}\,|\,{0<\nu}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}^{u}}{}{\,\shortrightarrow\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{{\tt Last}({\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt pushI}}}\;{0\;0}}{\rangle})}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,{\tt unit}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt push}}\;{x}}{\rangle}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt pushI}}}\;{1\;x}}{\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}

(by
SubG
)

The rule
TOpHis
can then be used to specialize its history
trace to align with that of the actual calling context
(
{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt pushI}}}\;{0\;0}}{\rangle}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt popI}}}\;{0\;0}}{\rangle}
). Note that we do not
need to also specialize the future regex, since it is already a
prefix of the future trace of the target type:

\displaystyle...\vdash\boldsymbol{{\tt push}}:x{:}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{}{{\nu}{:}{{\tt int}}\,|\,{0<\nu}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}^{u}}{}{\,\shortrightarrow\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt pushI}}}\;{0\;0}}{\rangle}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt popI}}}\;{0\;0}}{\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,{\tt unit}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt push}}\;{x}}{\rangle}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt pushI}}}\;{1\;x}}{\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}

(by
TOpHis
)

The rest of program is now typed via the following judgement:

\displaystyle x{:}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{}{{\nu}{:}{{\tt int}}\,|\,{0<\nu}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}^{u}}{}\vdash\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}:{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt pushI}}}\;{0\;0}}{\rangle}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt popI}}}\;{0\;0}}{\rangle}{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt push}}\;{x}}{\rangle}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt pushI}}}\;{1\;x}}{\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,x{:}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{}{{\nu}{:}{{\tt int}}\,|\,{0<\nu}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}^{u}}{}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}\;{x}}{\rangle}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt popI}}}\;{1\;x}}{\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}

As before, after looking up the type of
\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}
using

TOpCtx
(
①
), we can instantiate the ghost variable
n
as
1

and
m
as
0
via
SubG
(
②
), align the return type via

SubHF
((
③
) and refine the history regex via the rule

TOpHis
(
④
):

\displaystyle...\vdash\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}:n{:}{\tt int}{\,\dashrightarrow\,}m{:}{\tt int}{\,\dashrightarrow\,}

①

\displaystyle\hskip 17.00024pt\hskip 17.00024pt\ {\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{{\tt Last}({\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt popI}}}\;{m\;\_}}{\rangle})\land{\tt Last}({\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt pushI}}}\;{n\;\_}}{\rangle})\land({\bullet^{*}}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt pushI}}}\;{(n{-}m)\;x}}{\rangle}{\bullet^{*}})}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,x{:}{\tt int}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}\;{x}}{\rangle}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt popI}}}\;{m{+}1\;x}}{\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}

②

\displaystyle...\vdash\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}:{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{{\tt Last}({\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt popI}}}\;{0\;\_}}{\rangle})\land{\tt Last}({\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt pushI}}}\;{1\;\_}}{\rangle})\land({\bullet^{*}}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt pushI}}}\;{1\;x}}{\rangle}{\bullet^{*}})}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,x{:}{\tt int}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}\;{x}}{\rangle}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt popI}}}\;{1\;x}}{\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}

③

\displaystyle...\vdash\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}:{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{{\tt Last}({\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt popI}}}\;{0\;\_}}{\rangle})\land{\tt Last}({\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt pushI}}}\;{1\;\_}}{\rangle})\land({\bullet^{*}}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt pushI}}}\;{1\;x}}{\rangle}{\bullet^{*}})}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,x{:}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{}{{\nu}{:}{{\tt int}}\,|\,{0<\nu}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}^{u}}{}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}\;{x}}{\rangle}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt popI}}}\;{1\;x}}{\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}

④

\displaystyle...\vdash\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}:{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt pushI}}}\;{0\;0}}{\rangle}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt popI}}}\;{0\;0}}{\rangle}{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt push}}\;{x}}{\rangle}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt pushI}}}\;{1\;x}}{\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,x{:}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{}{{\nu}{:}{{\tt int}}\,|\,{0<\nu}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}^{u}}{}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}\;{x}}{\rangle}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt popI}}}\;{1\;x}}{\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}

3.3.
Metatheory

Although ghost events constrain
u
Hat
s, they do not
appear in actual traces produced by the operational semantics. We
define an erasure function
\lceil{\alpha}\rceil
to remove ghost events from a trace
\alpha
.

Type denotations

Similar to other refinement type
systems
(
Jhala and Vazou 2021
)
, types in
\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{C}
are denoted as their inhabitants
(i.e.,
\llbracket{t}\rrbracket
and
\llbracket{\tau}\rrbracket
). The type context
\Gamma
is
denoted as a
substitution

\sigma
(i.e.,

[x_{1}\mapsto v_{1},x_{2}\mapsto v_{2}]
) that provides the assignments for
binding variables in
\Gamma
and the denotation (the denoted
language) of SREs is a set of traces. Then, trace inclusion under a
type context is defined as

\Gamma\vdash A\subseteq A^{\prime}\doteq\forall\sigma\in\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket.\llbracket{\sigma(A)}\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket{\sigma(A^{\prime})}\rrbracket
. The
type denotation of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{H}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,x{:}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{}{{\nu}{:}{b}\,|\,{\phi}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}^{u}}{}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{F}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}
is:

\displaystyle\{e~|~\emptyset\vdash_{{\tt s}}e:b\land\forall v{:}b.\phi[x\mapsto v]{\implies}\forall\alpha_{f}\in\llbracket{F[x\mapsto v]}\rrbracket.\exists\alpha_{h}\in\llbracket{H[x\mapsto v]}\rrbracket.{\lceil{\alpha_{h}}\rceil}\vDash{e}{\,\xhookrightarrow{\lceil{\alpha_{f}}\rceil}\,\hskip-5.0pt^{*}\,}{v}\}

A term
e
inhabits
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{H}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,x{:}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{}{{\nu}{:}{b}\,|\,{\phi}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}^{u}}{}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{F}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}
iff, for trace
\alpha_{f}
accepted by
F
, there exists history trace
\alpha_{h}
accepted by
H
, such that the execution of
e
under

\alpha_{h}
produces
\alpha_{f}
.
Our formulation constitutes a trace-based analogue of IL
(
O’Hearn 2019
)
, in which effects are modeled as program state transitions:

\displaystyle[P]\;e\;[Q]\doteq\forall s_{\tt post}\in\llbracket{Q}\rrbracket.\exists s_{\tt pre}\in\llbracket{P}\rrbracket.(s_{\tt pre},e)\Downarrow s_{\tt post}

A notable distinction between these two presentations is that we
characterize
F
as the set of
newly produced
traces rather
than the full post-execution trace, reflecting our trace-based
type rules. This formulation additionally avoids
redundant prefixes inherited from the execution history.

We expect the
u
Hat
s provided by the operator typing context

\Delta
to be consistent with the corresponding auxiliary operator
semantics:

Definition 3.3 (Well-formed operator typing context).

The operator typing context
\Delta
is well-formed
iff the semantics of every operator
\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}
is
consistent with the type
\overline{x{:}b_{\mathit{x}}}{\,\dashrightarrow\,}\overline{y{:}t_{\mathit{y}}}{\,\shortrightarrow\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{H}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,z{:}t\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{F}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}
provided by
\Delta
, and
H

does not specify any unreachable traces.

\displaystyle\overline{\forall x{:}b_{\mathit{x}}}.\;\overline{\forall y\in\llbracket{t_{\mathit{y}}}\rrbracket}.\;\forall H^{\prime}.H^{\prime}\not=\emptyset\land H^{\prime}\subseteq H{\implies}(\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}\,\overline{y})\in\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{H^{\prime}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,z{:}t\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{F}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}}\rrbracket

Theorem 3.4 (Fundamental Theorem).

A well-typed term, i.e.,

\Gamma\vdash e:{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{H}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,t\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{F}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}
, generates traces consistent
with its
u
Hat
:

\forall\sigma\in\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket.\sigma(e)\in\llbracket{\sigma({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{H}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,t\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{F}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{})}\rrbracket
.

Corollary 3.5 (Type Soundness).

A generator
e
that satisfies

\emptyset\vdash e:{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,{\tt unit}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{F}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}
must produce all
traces accepted by
F
, i.e.,

\forall\alpha\in\llbracket{F}\rrbracket.{[]}\vDash{e}{\,\xhookrightarrow{\lceil{\alpha}\rceil}\,\hskip-5.0pt^{*}\,}{()}
.

4.
Synthesis

Given an SRE
A
describing some property of a black-box system under
test, Corollary
3.5
ensures that a well-typed test generator
program
\emptyset\vdash e:{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,{\tt unit}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{F}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}

must

be able to produce
every
trace
\alpha
that is consistent with
both
F
and
A
, i.e.
\alpha\in F\land A
. Thus, the high-level
goal of our synthesis procedure is to find a generator that a) uses
effectful operators in a way that is consistent with the temporal and
data-dependency constraints captured by their specifications in

\Delta
, and b) has a
u
Hat
whose future automaton shares a
meaningful overlap with
A
. Our synthesis algorithm simultaneously
solves both problems by using a loop, depicted in

Fig. 6
, to iteratively
refine
the target SRE
into a set of more concrete SREs, each of which can be directly mapped
to a well-typed generator program. Each iteration of this loop targets
a single event in an element of this set and adds events before and
after that event so that its dependencies are satisfied, ensuring that
the corresponding operator in a synthesized program is well-typed. Our
loop also employs a regex non-emptiness check to ensure that each
element of this set represents a generator that produces at least one
feasible trace. After the refinement loop has finished, a well-typed
generator program can be mechanically extracted using the refined
property as a template.

Figure 6
.
Clouseau
’s high-level test generator synthesis algorithm

4.1.
Synthesis Algorithm

The main refinement loop in our synthesis algorithm operates over

abstract traces
— sequences of symbolic events

{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}~|~{\phi}}{\rangle}
plus alternatives defined by the following grammar:

\displaystyle\text{{Abstract Trace}}\hskip 9.24994pt\pi::=\epsilon~|~{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}~|~{\phi}}{\rangle}^{b}~|~\pi{\cdot}\pi~|~[\overline{{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}~|~{\phi}}{\rangle}^{b}}]^{*}

Each symbolic event
{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}~|~{\phi}}{\rangle}^{b}
in
an abstract trace is tagged with a boolean
b
indicating whether the
surrounding context satisfies its constraints. Operating over abstract
traces helps our synthesizer easily identify events with unresolved
dependencies. Every SRE can be normalized into a set of abstract
traces via an algorithm that applies a standard minimization algorithm
and then decomposes unions into abstract traces:

{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt op_{1}}}~|~{\phi_{1}}}{\rangle}\cup{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt op_{2}}}~|~{\phi_{2}}}{\rangle}
becomes

\{{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt op_{1}}}~|~{\phi_{1}}}{\rangle}^{\bot},{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt op_{2}}}~|~{\phi_{2}}}{\rangle}^{\bot}\}
, for
example.
6
6

6

The full definition of our SRE normalization
procedure is provided in the supplemental material.

Example 4.1 (SRE Normalization).

The set of abstract traces corresponding to the history regex of

\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}
from Example
3.1
includes the
following abstract trace:

Original SRE:

\displaystyle{\tt Last}({\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt popI}}}\;{m\;\_}}{\rangle})\land{\tt Last}({\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt pushI}}}\;{n\;\_}}{\rangle})\land({\bullet^{*}}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt pushI}}}\;{(n{-}m)\;x}}{\rangle}{\bullet^{*}})

(
\pi_{\tt pop1}
)

One normalized trace:

\displaystyle\Pi_{\tt any}^{*}{\cdot}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt popI}}}\;{m\;\_}}{\rangle}^{\bot}{\cdot}\Pi_{\tt noPopI}^{*}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt pushI}}}\;{(n-m)\;x}}{\rangle}^{\bot}{\cdot}\Pi_{\tt noPopI}^{*}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt pushI}}}\;{n\;\_}}{\rangle}^{\bot}{\cdot}\Pi_{\tt noI}^{*}

where

\displaystyle\Pi_{\tt any}\equiv[{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}pushI}}}}{\rangle}^{\bot}\mid{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}popI}}}}{\rangle}^{\bot}\mid{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt push}}}{\rangle}^{\bot}\mid{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}}{\rangle}^{\bot}]

\displaystyle\Pi_{\tt noPopI}\equiv[{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}pushI}}}}{\rangle}^{\bot}\mid{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt push}}}{\rangle}^{\bot}\mid{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}}{\rangle}^{\bot}]\hskip 17.00024pt\hskip 8.50012pt\Pi_{\tt noI}\equiv[{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt push}}}{\rangle}^{\bot}\mid{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}}{\rangle}^{\bot}]

This abstract trace imposes an ordering on the three intersected
symbolic events in the original SRE; other traces correspond to
different orderings. The alternatives after

{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt popI}}}\;{m\;\_}}{\rangle}
and
{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt pushI}}}\;{(n-m)\;x}}{\rangle}
in the
trace disallow
{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}popI}}}}{\rangle}
event and

{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}pushI}}}}{\rangle}
events, respectively. The minimization step
also produces traces that merge these intersected events: the
procedure automatically considers the case where
m=0
, which
unifies the two
\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}pushI}}}
events, resulting in the inclusion
of the following abstract trace in the normalized set:

(
\pi_{\tt pop2}
)

\displaystyle\Pi_{\tt any}^{*}{\cdot}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt popI}}}\;{0\;\_}}{\rangle}^{\bot}{\cdot}\Pi_{\tt noPopI}^{*}{\cdot}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt pushI}}}\;{n\;x}}{\rangle}^{\bot}{\cdot}\Pi_{\tt noI}^{*}

Inputs :
Operator context
\Delta
, variables
\overline{x{:}b}
, and
target trace property
A

Output :
Generator
e
, such that

\overline{x{:}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{}{{\nu}{:}{b}\,|\,{\top}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}^{u}}{}}\vdash e:{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,{\tt unit}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{A^{\prime}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}\land\overline{x{:}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{}{{\nu}{:}{b}\,|\,{\top}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}^{u}}{}}\vdash A^{\prime}\subseteq A

\Gamma\leftarrow\overline{x{:}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{}{{\nu}{:}{b}\,|\,{\top}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}^{u}}{}}
;

//
initial
type context

C\leftarrow\{(\Gamma,\pi)~\mid~\pi\in\mathbf{Norm}(A)\}

;

//
initialize set of abstract traces

while

\exists(\Gamma^{\prime},\pi)\in C.\;\pi=\pi_{h}{\cdot}{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}~|~{\phi}}{\rangle}^{\bot}{\cdot}\pi_{f}

do

//
events still need to be refined

1

\Pi\leftarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt Refine}}(\Delta,\Gamma^{\prime},\pi_{h},{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}~|~{\phi}}{\rangle}^{\bot},\pi_{f})\cup C-\{(\Gamma^{\prime},\pi)\}

return

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt DeriveTerm}}(C)
;

//
synthesize recursions and derive generator program

Algorithm 1

Test Generator Synthesis

Inputs :
Operator context
\Delta
, typing context
\Gamma
,
abstract trace
\pi_{h}{\cdot}{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}~|~{\phi}}{\rangle}^{\bot}{\cdot}\pi_{f}

Output :
Set of refined abstract traces
\Pi
where the
dependencies of
{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}~|~{\phi}}{\rangle}
are satisfied

\overline{z{:}b}{\,\dashrightarrow\,}\overline{y{:}t_{y}}{\,\shortrightarrow\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{H}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,x{:}t_{x}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}~|~{\phi^{\prime}}}{\rangle}{\cdot}F}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}\leftarrow\Delta(\boldsymbol{{\tt op}})
;

//
retrieve
u
Hat
of

\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}

\Gamma^{\prime}\leftarrow\Gamma,\overline{z{:}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{}{{\nu}{:}{b}\,|\,{\top}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}^{u}}{}},\overline{y{:}t_{y}},x{:}t_{x}
;

//
add ghost variables and
parameters types to type context

{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}~|~{\phi^{\prime\prime}}}{\rangle}\leftarrow{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}~|~{\phi\land\phi^{\prime}}}{\rangle}

;

//
refine target operation

H^{\prime}\leftarrow\pi_{h}\land H
;

//
merge history regex

F^{\prime}\leftarrow\pi_{f}\land(F{\cdot}{\bullet^{*}})
;

//
merge future
regex

P\leftarrow\mathbf{Norm}(H^{\prime}{\cdot}{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}~|~{\phi^{\prime\prime}}}{\rangle}^{\top}{\cdot}F^{\prime})

;

//
normalize refined trace

return

\{(\Gamma^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})\mid\pi^{\prime}\in P\land\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\pi^{\prime}\not\subseteq\emptyset\}

;

//
filter empty abstract traces

Algorithm 2

Abstract Trace Refinement (
Refine
)

Our top-level synthesis algorithm is shown in
Algorithm 1
.
Given a target trace property
A
whose qualifiers only contain the
variables
\overline{x{:}b}
, this algorithm synthesizes a well-typed

\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{C}{}
generator. The algorithm maintains a set of candidate
abstract traces,
C
, each of which is a refinement of
A
; this set
is initialized directly from
A
(line 2). Each trace in
C
is paired
with a typing context that the synthesizer uses to filter out
refinements that do not correspond to well-typed programs. The
algorithm first uses a loop (lines
3-4
) to refine
C
until it only
contains abstract traces that are consistent with the operator context

\Delta
, and then uses the resulting set of traces to derive the
final (recursive) generator (line
5
). Each iteration of this loop
nondeterministically chooses a target event in one of the current
abstract traces (line
2
) whose tag indicates its dependencies are
not satisfied; it then uses the
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2773,0.6719}{\tt Refine}}
subroutine to insert the
required operations before and after the target event.

Abstract trace refinement

The abstract trace refinement
subroutine,
Refine
, is shown in
Algorithm 2
. It first
retrieves the
u
Hat
of the target operation
\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}
from

\Delta
(line
1
), and adds its parameters to the type context (line

3
).
Refine
then merges the selected
u
Hat
with the current
abstract trace (lines
4-6
) and normalizes the result to produce a
set of refinements of the input trace that are consistent with

\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}
(line
7
). Line
6
effectively applies the

SubHF
rule to drop any states that are unreachable after the
target operation; while line 4 effectively applies
TOpHis
to
ensure the trace that precedes the target operation satisfies its
specification. Before returning the refined trace,
Refine
checks that it
corresponds to a meaningful test generator by filtering out any empty
traces (line
7
).

Example 4.2 (Abstract Trace Refinement).

We illustrate the refinement process by showing how it derives a
trace corresponding to the generator from in
Example
3.2
. Assume the refinement
loop begins with the following (singleton) set of abstract traces:

\displaystyle\Pi_{\tt any}^{*}{\cdot}{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}~|~{\top}}{\rangle}{\cdot}\Pi_{\tt any}^{*}

where the user requires that the test generator performs at least
one
\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}
operation. In the first iteration, the symbolic
event
{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}~|~{\top}}{\rangle}
is selected for refinement using

Refine
. The algorithm first retrieves the
u
Hat
of

\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}
from
\Delta
:

\displaystyle\Delta(\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}})\equiv n{:}{\tt int}{\,\dashrightarrow\,}m{:}{\tt int}{\,\dashrightarrow\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{\pi_{\tt pop1}\cup\pi_{\tt pop2}\cup...}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,x{:}{\tt int}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}\;{x}}{\rangle}{\cdot}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt popI}}}\;{m{+}1\;x}}{\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\cdot}\Pi_{\tt any}^{*}

We have normalized the history regex to illustrate that
merging this
u
Hat
with the current abstract trace and
normalizing can result in multiple new abstract traces. For
example, property

π
pop2

in the history regex can yield
the following trace:

\displaystyle(m{:}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{}{{\nu}{:}{{\tt int}}\,|\,{\nu=0}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}^{u}}{},n{:}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{}{{\nu}{:}{{\tt int}}\,|\,{\top}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}^{u}}{},y{:}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{}{{\nu}{:}{{\tt int}}\,|\,{\top}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}^{u}}{},x{:}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{}{{\nu}{:}{{\tt int}}\,|\,{\top}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}^{u}}{},

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\Pi_{\tt any}^{*}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt popI}}}\;{m\;y}}{\rangle}^{\bot}\Pi_{\tt noPopI}^{*}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt pushI}}}\;{n\;x}}{\rangle}^{\bot}\Pi_{\tt noI}^{*}{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}\;{x}}{\rangle}^{\top}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt popI}}}\;{m\;x}}{\rangle}^{\bot})

The new variables in the type context require
m=0
, as
mentioned in Example
4.1
. Although the
u
Hat
of

\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}
does not explicitly add any new
\boldsymbol{{\tt push}}
or

\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}
events to the abstract trace, it does require multiple
ghost events, which will recover corresponding concrete events during
further refinement. For example, the
u
Hat
of
\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}pushI}}}

is given below; it requires that a
\boldsymbol{{\tt pushI}}
event always occurs
after a
\boldsymbol{{\tt push}}
event, unless it is an initialization event
(i.e.,
i=0
):

\displaystyle\Delta(\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}pushI}}})\equiv

\displaystyle\;i{:}{\tt int}{\,\shortrightarrow\,}x{:}{\tt int}{\,\shortrightarrow\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{{\bullet^{*}}{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt push}}}{\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,{\tt unit}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt pushI}}}\;{i\;x}~|~{i>0}}{\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}\,\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\sqcap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{(\bullet\setminus{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}pushI}}}}{\rangle})^{*}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,{\tt unit}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt pushI}}}\;{i\;x}~|~{i=0}}{\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}

\displaystyle\Delta(\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}popI}}})\equiv

\displaystyle\;i{:}{\tt int}{\,\shortrightarrow\,}x{:}{\tt int}{\,\shortrightarrow\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{{\bullet^{*}}{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}}{\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,{\tt unit}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt popI}}}\;{i\;x}~|~{i>0}}{\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}\,\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\sqcap}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{(\bullet\setminus{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}popI}}}}{\rangle})^{*}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,{\tt unit}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt popI}}}\;{i\;x}~|~{i=0}}{\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}

On the other hand, the
{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt popI}}}\;{m\;y}}{\rangle}
event in the abstract trace has index
m=0
, and the algorithm can smartly determine that there is no previous
\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}
event in this case. It is because the merge result of the first intersected type of
\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}popI}}}
fails the non-emptiness check, where
m>0\land m=0
is unsatisfiable. Finally, this trace will be refined to one consistent with the program shown in Example
3.2
:

\displaystyle(m{:}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{}{{\nu}{:}{{\tt int}}\,|\,{\nu=0}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}^{u}}{},n{:}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{}{{\nu}{:}{{\tt int}}\,|\,{\nu=m{+}1}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}^{u}}{},y{:}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{}{{\nu}{:}{{\tt int}}\,|\,{\top}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}^{u}}{},x{:}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{}{{\nu}{:}{{\tt int}}\,|\,{\nu>y}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}^{u}}{},

(
\pi_{\tt stack}
)

\displaystyle\;\Pi_{\tt noI}^{*}{\cdot}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt pushI}}}\;{m\;y}}{\rangle}^{\bot}{\cdot}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt popI}}}\;{m\;y}}{\rangle}^{\bot}{\cdot}\Pi_{\tt noI}^{*}{\cdot}{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt push}}\;{x}}{\rangle}^{\bot}{\cdot}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt pushI}}}\;{n\;x}}{\rangle}^{\bot}{\cdot}\Pi_{\tt noI}^{*}{\cdot}{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}\;{x}}{\rangle}^{\top}{\cdot}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt popI}}}\;{n\;x}}{\rangle}^{\bot}{\cdot}\Pi_{\tt any}^{*})

Deriving a Generator

The second component of our synthesis algorithm,
DeriveTerm
, derives
generators for each abstract trace in the set produced the refinement
loop and then combines them using
\oplus
to construct the final
generator. This process relies on a subroutine,
DeriveTrace
, that
generates straightline programs without recursion by dropping by all
ghost events and variables and then mapping each symbolic event to a
corresponding effectful operation with appropriate arguments. So,
given the following context and abstract trace:

\displaystyle(m{:}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{}{{\nu}{:}{{\tt int}}\,|\,{\nu=0}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}^{u}}{},n{:}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{}{{\nu}{:}{{\tt int}}\,|\,{\nu=m{+}1}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}^{u}}{},y{:}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{}{{\nu}{:}{{\tt int}}\,|\,{\top}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}^{u}}{},x{:}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{}{{\nu}{:}{{\tt int}}\,|\,{\nu>y}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}^{u}}{},{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt push}}\;{x}}{\rangle}{\cdot}{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}\;{x}}{\rangle})

DeriveTrace
will synthesize the following program:

⬇

1

let

(
x
:

int
)

=

?
$
\
mykw
{
assume
}\;

(\
exists

m
\;
n
\;
y
.

m

=

0

\
land

n

=

m
{+}1

\
land

x

>

y
)
$
?

in

push

x
;

(*

equivalent

to

assume

true

*)

2

let

(
z
:

int
)

=

pop

()

in

assert

(
x

==

z
)

DeriveTerm
applies a sketch-style methodology to synthesize a recursive
generator, using
DeriveTrace
to fill in the holes of a template
recursive function definition. It does so by associating each of the
holes in the template with a subsequence of the abstract trace,
treating each occurence of a Kleene star in an abstract trace as a
candidate placeholder for a recursive call. In order to ensure a
particular partitioning is sensible, the algorithm simulates a bounded
number of loop iterations to ensure a completed sketch is well-typed.

Example 4.3 (Recursion Template).

Consider the following template which defines and uses a recursive
function
f
that calls itself once:

T\doteq{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{let}}}}}\ {f}\ {=}\ {{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{fix}}}}}\ f.\lambda().\ ()\oplus\boxed{e_{1}};f\;();\boxed{e_{2}}}\ {\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{in}}}}}\ {(\boxed{e_{3}};f\;();\boxed{e_{4}})}
. This
template includes a pair of holes before and after the recursive
call in the definition of
f
, and another pair around the top-level
call to
f
. We can build the following two program sketches by
mapping portions of
\pi_{\tt stack}
from
Example
4.2
to the four holes in
T
:

\displaystyle\underbrace{\Pi_{\tt noI}^{*}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt pushI}}}\;{m\;y}}{\rangle}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt popI}}}\;{m\;y}}{\rangle}\Pi_{\tt noI}^{*}}_{e_{3}}\;\;\underbrace{{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt push}}\;{x}}{\rangle}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt pushI}}}\;{n\;x}}{\rangle}}_{e_{1}}\;\;\underbrace{\Pi_{\tt noI}^{*}}_{\text{$f$\;()}}\;\;\underbrace{{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}\;{x}}{\rangle}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt popI}}}\;{n\;x}}{\rangle}}_{e_{2}}\;\;\underbrace{\Pi_{\tt noI}^{*}}_{e_{4}}

\displaystyle\underbrace{\Pi_{\tt noI}^{*}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt pushI}}}\;{m\;y}}{\rangle}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt popI}}}\;{m\;y}}{\rangle}\Pi_{\tt noI}^{*}{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt push}}\;{x}}{\rangle}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt pushI}}}\;{n\;x}}{\rangle}}_{e_{3}}\;\;\underbrace{\Pi_{\tt noI}^{*}}_{e_{1}}\;\;\underbrace{\Pi_{\tt noI}^{*}}_{\text{$f$\;()}}\;\;\underbrace{{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}\;{x}}{\rangle}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt popI}}}\;{n\;x}}{\rangle}}_{e_{2}}\;\;\underbrace{\Pi_{\tt noI}^{*}}_{e_{4}}

We can then simulate unrolling the recursive call in a solution to
each sketch by duplicating the subtraces labeled
e_{1}\;f\;()\;e_{2}
a bounded number of times and checking whether they are consistent with

\pi_{\tt stack}
: doing so reveals that the second sketch would
not yield a valid solution, as the recursive call would repeatedly
perform
\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}
events without any
\boldsymbol{{\tt push}}
events. The
first sketch, in contrast, is consistent with
\pi_{\tt stack}
;
using
DeriveTrace
to synthesize generators for each
e
yields
the following:

⬇

1

let

rec

f

()

=

()

?
$
\
oplus$
?

2

let

(
x
:

int
)

=

int_gen

()

in

push

x
;

(*

?
$e_1$
?

*)

3

f

();

4

let

(
z
:

int
)

=

pop

()

in

assert

(
x

==

z
)

(*

?
$e_2$
?

*)

5

in

(*

?
$e_3$
?

*)

f

()

(*

?
$e_4$
?

*)

Theorem 4.4 (
Algorithm 1
is Sound).

Every generator synthesized by
Algorithm 1
is type-safe
with respect to our declarative typing system.

5.
Implementation And Evaluation

We have implemented a tool based on the above approach, called

Clouseau
, that synthesizes effectful test generators from user-provided

u
Hat
specifications and property. The output program in our DSL
can be transpiled to OCaml (QCheck) or P. Our experiments use the
OCaml transpilation to test effectful abstract datatype libraries,
serializers, transformers, and interpreters, concurrent data
structures and databases; we use the P target to test reactive
distributed system models (i.e., message-passing systems defined as a
collection of actors) by using the synthesized generator as a
property-directed scheduler that replaces P’s default random one.
Notably, the same specification and synthesis strategy is used in both
cases.
Clouseau
consists of approximately 14K lines of OCaml code and
uses Z3
(
de Moura and Bjørner 2008
)
as its backend SMT solver.

Evaluation setting

Clouseau
takes two inputs: a target
property, expressed as an SRE, and an operator context that contains

u
Hat
specifications for the operators used in the SUT. During
synthesis,
Clouseau
decomposes the target property to build a generator
consistent with the traces that the property accepts, but constrained
to respect the history and future traces of any effects it
references. Because operator types are interpreted
underapproximately, there are potentially many valid candidate
programs that are consistent with the
u
Hat
specifications of the
operators they use. Our implementation considers a union of
well-typed synthesis candidates, the number of which is bounded by the
amount of time allotted for synthesis (3 minutes in our experiments).
Synthesized generators use
{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{assume}}}}}
statements to ensure that
data dependencies among effectful operations are respected and use

{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{assert}}}}}
statements to flag property violations.
Like other PBT setups that rely on precondition-style filtering, our
evaluation adopts an
angelic
reading of
{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{assume}}}}}
: when
the SUT or runtime rejects a candidate trace (e.g., when the
interpreter aborts on an ill-typed expression), we treat that run as
uninformative and
retry
with a fresh sample rather than
counting it toward a found violation. This matches how generators
are typically used in practice and avoids conflating specification
imprecision with genuine safety failures.

Our experimental evaluation addresses four research questions:

Q1
:

Is
Clouseau

expressive
? Can it synthesize
generators for a diverse set of benchmarks with interesting
non-trivial structural and semantic safety properties?

Q2
:

Is
Clouseau

effective
? Do synthesized
generators enable more targeted exploration of the state
space to witness violations of provided safety properties than
existing techniques?

Q3
:

Is
Clouseau

efficient
? Is it able to
synthesize meaningful generators reasonably quickly?

Q4
:

Is
Clouseau

robust
? Is it
able to synthesize meaningful generators for benchmarks even when
provided with weaker specifications than necessary?

Table 1
.
Using
Clouseau
to synthesize effectful test
generators in OCaml. Benchmarks from prior work are annotated with
their source: HAT
(
Zhou et al. 2024
)
, the OCaml multicore PBT
framework
(

)
, QuickCheck on IFC

(

)
and well-typed De Bruijn STLC

, an extensible testbed for

(

)
, a

baselines used for comparison are taken from the original sources
when they exist (these are labeled with
†
), otherwise
we use QCheck’s default random generator.
Clouseau
synthesized
correct-by-construction test generators for all benchmarks within
3 minutes; the number of generator variants synthesized is bounded
at 3. We set a 30-minute bound for the baselines to find a
property violation.

Table 2
.
Experimental results of using
Clouseau
to synthesize
property-directed schedulers for reactive distributed
systems. Benchmarks from prior work are annotated with their

(

)
(

(

□

are given as

s.
Clouseau
synthesizes a set
of schedulers, each of which specifies a distinct scheduling
order for messages, all consistent with provided specifications.

3

(t
{}_{\text{total}}
is the average time to find a single
controller.) We set a bound of 30 minute time bound for the P
baselines to generate a faulty execution.
