We have evaluated
Clouseau
on a diverse corpus of programs
7
7

7

The
supplemental material includes additional details about our
benchmarks.
drawn from a variety of sources (described in the captions of

Table 1
and
Table 2
) (
Q1
). Our benchmarks are grouped into four categories.
The first are data structure benchmarks (
Stack
,
Set
,

Filesystem
) where the test generator probes for violations of
structural invariants. The second are evaluators - programs that
input serialized data, transform them into an internal AST
representation, and then interpret them; we consider evaluators for
data structures like graphs and automata (
Graph
,

NFA
), information flow control machines, and simply-typed
lambda-calculus, the latter two both described
in
Sec. 2
. The third are concurrent and database
benchmarks (
Transaction
,
Shopping
,

Courseware
,
Twitter
,
Smallbank
) that test
properties related to consistency and synchronization. Property
violations in any of these categories only manifest in specific
configurations (e.g., a violating test case for
NFA
involves
constructing an automata containing multiple nodes with more than two
outgoing edges). We evaluate these three classes of benchmarks by
transpiling synthesized generators into OCaml, leveraging its QCheck
library as needed. The SUT for each of these benchmarks is known to be
buggy; in all cases we use implementations either available from prior
sources directly (if the OCaml source was available), or by directly
translating them into OCaml. The fourth group of benchmarks shown
in
Table 2
considers applications involving various
kinds of distributed protocols; these are executed using the P runtime
environment, by transpiling synthesized generators written in our DSL
into P’s state machine modeling language. Here as well, the protocol
being tested is known to be faulty from the sources they were adopted
from. The
IFCLoad
W
,

DeBruijnHO
W
, and

Transaction
W
benchmarks are variants of other
benchmarks that use the weaker specifications described in

Sec. 2
; the end of this section discusses these
results in more detail (
Q4
).

Table 1
divides the results of our experiments into
four categories, separated by double bars. The first measures the
complexity of benchmarks with respect to the number of distinct
effectful operators (#
\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}
) and the number of qualifiers (i.e.,
symbolic events) used in
u
Hat
specifications and the property
being tested. For the benchmarks in
Table 1
, our
results show that writing underapproximate specifications require
anywhere from
6
-
22
different operators and include over 100
symbolic events in our most complex examples. The second group of
columns characterizes
Clouseau
’s ability to discover a violation
compared to the baseline (
Q2
). To make the comparison
objective, we execute generators in the two systems 10K times, and
count the number of runs in which a fault was uncovered. For example,
for
Stack
, using QCheck to generate random sequences of

\boldsymbol{{\tt push}}
and
\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}
effects, resulted in an erroneous
execution every 31.9 times on average; in contrast, every execution of
the generator(s) synthesized by
Clouseau
manifested the bug. Notably,
even for benchmarks in which there is a sophisticated customized
generator available
8
8

8

When these custom generators are
available, we use them as the baseline in our experiments.
(e.g.,

IFC
or
de Bruijn
),
u
Hat
-guided synthesis leads
to significantly better outcomes. We use MonkeyDB
(
Biswas et al. 2021
)
, a
mock storage system that can be used to find violations of database
isolation properties, as the baseline for a number of the concurrency
benchmarks. Although it is specialized for this particular
application class, and
Clouseau
is not, evaluation results are
comparable, with
Clouseau
yielding generators that identify weak
isolation violations modestly more effectively than the baseline.
Similar conclusions apply to the P benchmarks shown
in
Table 2
. For simple benchmarks, P’s default
scheduler, which performs enumerative state exploration to construct
schedules, independently of the behaviors of the actors under test or
the target property, or handwritten ones which inject additional
actors to control input message generation and prevent uninteresting
message orderings (e.g.
Simplified2PC
or
Heartbeat
),
are effective and
Clouseau
’s performance is comparable to these. As in
the previous set of experiments, however, for more complex benchmarks
(e.g.,
ChainReplication
,
Paxos
), even manual
specialization to generate interesting test sequences is significantly
less effective than
Clouseau
’s trace-guided synthesis approach.
Moreover, as indicated by the
Raft
numbers, a random
exploration procedure, without manually engineered guidance, is too
naïve to find a violating execution. It is notable that here too,

Clouseau
’s performance on these P benchmarks is competitive, and in
several cases, superior to handcrafted P schedulers, even though

Clouseau
has no built-in knowledge of P’s underlying programming model.

The last group of columns in both tables provide details on the cost
of our synthesis procedure. The first column presents total synthesis
time (t
{}_{\text{total}}
), which takes anywhere from
.6
to
165

seconds (
Q3
); synthesis time is roughly proportional to
benchmark complexity, as reflected in the #
\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}
and #qualifier
columns. The last three columns additionally analyze the
characteristics of
Clouseau
’s synthesis procedure with respect to the
number of events in the abstract refined trace (#evt), the number of
SMT queries (#SMT), as well as the number of refinement steps
(#refine); recall that the synthesis procedure uses a refinement loop
to construct a more specialized underapproximation from
u
Hat

specifications that are consistent with the provided safety property.
In general, these numbers correlate with each other, and serve as
rough proxies for benchmark complexity. They indicate that even for
the most challenging benchmarks,
Clouseau
is able to synthesize an
effective generator in a reasonable amount of time.

Case study

To demonstrate that
Clouseau
can be effective in real-world scenarios,
we have applied it to
AnonReadAtomicity
, a distributed
transaction system in use at a major cloud provider, where
asynchronous reads are expected to execute atomically, as discussed
in
Sec. 2
. The property to be checked requires that if
there exists a key
{\tt k}
updated within an active transaction,
any successful read response asking its value should return the value
last written to
{\tt k}
made by that transaction.
Generating a fault-inducing scenario requires (a) initiating a new
transaction with transaction id
{\tt tid}
, (b) successfully
performing a write within that transaction, and then (c) subsequently
performing a read within
{\tt tid}
that yields a different value
than the one last written. As the example in
2

implies, using
u
Hat
to express the behavior of
\boldsymbol{{\tt readRsp}}

enables the synthesis of a test generator that can strategically
request a new transaction to initiate triggering the intended
violation. A version of the benchmark in which this sequence
structure is enforced manually discovers the violation in 53
executions, while
Clouseau
’s version can uncover the violation on all
of its executions.

Robustness to Specification Quality

As
discussed in
Sec. 2
,
Clouseau
’s effectiveness depends
on the quality of user-supplied
u
Hat
specifications. To
quantify
Clouseau
’s robustness to specification quality, we conducted
an additional study using variants of the benchmarks in
Tables
1
and
2
that keep the
SUT and the global property fixed while weakening the
u
Hat

specifications of individual operators provided by the SUT.
Specifications were weakened in a similar manner to the examples
described in
Sec. 2
: the variant of the IFC
benchmark (
IFCLoad
W
), for example, eliminates
the “valid address” constraint (i.e.,
\mathit{isAddr}
) used in the
original specification. Broadly speaking, the weakened
specifications can be categorized as either relaxing basic
correctness properties of the SUT or removing search biases. Both
categories of specifications are
lower-quality
because they
provide less guidance to
Clouseau
and thus increase the risk that it
will synthesize generators misaligned with the SUT’s intended
behavior.

Table 1
includes the results of this
experiment for the three examples from
Sec. 2

(
Transaction
W
,

IFCLoad
W
, and

DeBruijnHO
W
).
9
9

9

The experimental
results for the full set of benchmarks are provided in the
supplemental material.
Under these weaker specifications,
average synthesis time (t
{}_{\text{total}}
) improves by
18.18\%
, and
by
78.07\%
in the extreme case (
ChainReplication
). This
comes at the cost of decreased testing effectiveness, however:

26.9\%
(7 out of 26) of the benchmarks now time out; the remaining
benchmarks show an elevated retry rate (#Num. Executions),

1.9\times
on average, and
7.5\times
in the worst case
(
IFCLoad
W
). In general, weakening basic
correctness properties significantly impacts
Clouseau
’s
effectiveness, as the resulting generators regularly yield
executions that do not align with the intended behavior of the SUT:

DeBruijnHO
W
, for example, generates a large
number of “useless” untyped STLC programs that are immediately
discarded by the type-checker. Obtaining an informative witness from
such a generator may thus require generating many more inputs, and
can quickly exhaust the testing time budget. In contrast, weakening
the search bias was comparatively benign in our experiments (e.g.,
the synthesized generator for
IFCLoad
W
still
finds EENI violations relatively quickly), and primarily manifested
in an increased number of retries before a bug was found. Of
course, since weaker specifications can lead to shorter synthesis
times, the loss of testing efficiency may sometimes be an acceptable
trade-off.

Discussion

We have anecdotal evidence that the cost to write
u
Hat
s is
relatively low; the
u
Hat
s for the
HashTable
and

Courseware
benchmarks in
Table 1
were
written by an undergraduate student, and those for

Smallbank
and
Twitter
by two first-year PhD
students. None of the students had prior exposure to the
u
Hat

specification language or
Clouseau
’s synthesis algorithm. Nonetheless,
each was able to independently encode their knowledge about the SUT
as
u
Hat
s, requiring only a few person-hours to complete all the
benchmarks.

One potential area for future work is to provide better
developer-facing feedback when synthesis fails. Because our
algorithm consists of a refinement loop that maintains a set of
candidate generators (see
Fig. 6
), integrating a
refinement and debugging phase as part of the synthesizer and test
execution pipeline would be a natural extension.

6.
Related Work and Conclusions

Underapproximate Reasoning

A number of recently
proposed logical frameworks provide program reasoning principles based
on under-, rather than over-, approximate abstractions. Incorrectness
Logic
(
O’Hearn 2019
)
and related variants
(
Le et al. 2022
;
Raad et al. 2020
;
Zilberstein et al. 2023
)

present compositional proof rules that enable verification of
reachability (aka incorrectness) properties of first-order imperative
programs; their primary motivation is to enable formal reasoning of
(unsound) program analysis tools such as Infer
(
Distefano et al. 2019
)
. Ideas
related to these notions have also been incorporated into type
systems
(
Ramsay and Walpole 2024
;
Zhou et al. 2023
;
Jakob and Thiemann 2015
;
Li et al. 2026
)
for functional languages,
but none of these efforts have considered their application to the
synthesis of effectful test generators of the kind proposed in this
paper. For example
(
Zhou et al. 2023
)
uses underapproximation to
typecheck that a PBT generator is
complete
with respect to the
functional inputs it generates to the SUT, while
(
LaFontaine et al. 2025
)

considers an enumerative repair strategy to patch incomplete
generators so that they become complete. In contrast, in this paper,
we show how interpreting type specifications from the lens of
underapproximation can guide a synthesis procedure to construct
bespoke effectful test generators tailored to the property of
interest, informed by the behavior of the operators provided by the
SUT. As a mechanism to characterize underapproximate behavior, types
identify
sufficient
effectful constraints that any test input
sequence produced by the generator
must
satisfy based on these
specifications.

Specializing PBT Generators

There has been significant prior work to make PBT implementations more
effective, by providing tools that enable reasoning and customization
of effect traces produced by a test generator. For example,

Claessen et al. 2009
describes a customizable user-level scheduler that uses
Quviq QuickCheck’s support for state machine models to implement
deterministic replayable test inputs to an Erlang SUT.
Concuerror
(
Christakis et al. 2013
)
systematically explores process interleavings
in a concurrent Erlang program via program instrumentation and
preemption bounding. Quickstrom
(
O’Connor and Wickström 2022
)
uses specifications
of possible actions expressed in its Specstrom specification language,
a dialect of LTL, to dynamically choose the next input that the
generator should provide for interactive applications. In contrast,

Clouseau
’s type-guided synthesis strategy yields
generators influenced by both the global property of interest and the
local
u
Hat
specifications of the operations used by the SUT.
Importantly, the synthesis procedure is guided by the history and
future SREs of provided specifications to determine a semantically
meaningful ordering of effectful actions.

Type and Effect Systems

Type and effect systems that target
temporal
properties on the
sequences of effects that a program may
produce
is a
well-studied subject. For example,
Skalka and Smith 2004
presents a type and
effect system for reasoning about the shape of histories (i.e., finite
traces) of events embedded in a program.
Koskinen and Terauchi 2014
present a type
and effect system that additionally supports verification properties
of infinite traces, specified as Büchi automata. More recently,

Sekiyama and Unno 2023
have considered how to support richer
control flow structures, e.g., delimited continuations, in such an
effect system. Closest to our work are
Hoare Automata Types

(
HAT
s)
(
Zhou et al. 2024
)
, which integrate symbolic finite
automata into a refinement type system.
HAT
s enable reasoning
about stateful sequential programs structured as a functional core
interacting with opaque effectful libraries.
u
Hat
s revise

HAT
s by viewing them as underapproximate specifications -
every trace accepted by the future regex in a
u
Hat
is
justified by a trace accepted by its history.

Conclusions

This paper proposes
Clouseau
, a trace-guided synthesizer of effectful PBT
generators. Its key innovations are (i) the adaptation of SREs,
expressed as types, that define traces of effectful actions that occur
before and after the execution of an expression, as underapproximate
specifications, and (ii) the synthesis of bespoke test generators
derived from these specifications. Experimental results on a wide
range of applications show that
Clouseau
is significantly more
effective in identifying test input sequences that falsify a property
than the existing state-of-the-art.

Appendix A
Outlines of Supplemental Materials

The supplemental material is organized as follows.
The detailed description of STLC example is provided in
Appendix B
.
The complete set of rules for our operational semantics, basic typing, and declarative typing judgments are provided in
Appendix C
,
Appendix D
, and
Appendix E
. The type denotation is presented in
Appendix F
. Details of the auxiliary functions in our typing algorithm are given in
Appendix G
. Proofs of the theorems in our paper are provided in
Appendix H
. Finally,
Appendix I
presents the benchmark specification explanations, and
Appendix J
offers detailed results of our experiments.

Details in Overview

We introduce how to use
u
Hat
to specify the well-typedness of the De Bruijn STLC example introduced in the overview section.

|

ty

2

serialized_term

=

Figure 7
.
STLC terms in a serialized form.

We explicitly define the syntax of the serialized STLC terms in
Fig. 7
. The definitions of constants and variables are standard, and variables
carry their de Bruijn index. However, the usual function abstraction
constructor “
{\tt Abs\;{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{of}}}}}\;ty*term}
” is now represented
by two operations, “
{\tt Abs\;{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{of}}}}}\;ty}
” and
“
{\tt EndAbs}
”, where the function body is the term that is
generated between them. Similarly, the typical application constructor
“
{\tt App\;{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{of}}}}}\;term*term}
” is now captured by three
operations: “
{\tt App}
”, “
{\tt AppL}
”, and “
{\tt AppR}
”.
Here, “
{\tt App}
” begins the application, “
{\tt AppL}
” marks the beginning of
the argument, and “
{\tt AppR}
” signals the end of the application.

An example of a serialized STLC term is shown below, where we color
the corresponding parts of the term and the serialized term for better
readability.

STLC term:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\lambda{\tt int}}.{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}[0]}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1})}\;{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}3}

serialized STLC term:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{{\tt app}};{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\boldsymbol{{\tt abs}}({{\tt int}})};{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\boldsymbol{{\tt var}}({0})};{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\boldsymbol{{\tt endAbs}}};\boldsymbol{{\tt appL}};{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\boldsymbol{{\tt const}}({3})};\boldsymbol{{\tt appR}}

We denote de Bruijn indices with “
[...]
” to distinguish them from
constants. The interpreter accepts a
serialized lambda term and evaluates it to a value. In the example
above, the input sequence should be reduced as
(\lambda{\tt int}.[0])\;3\hookrightarrow 3
.

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{{\tt const}}:~

\displaystyle x{:}{\tt int}{\,\shortrightarrow\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{{\bullet^{*}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,{\tt unit}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt const}}\;{x}}{\rangle}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt ty}}}\;{{\tt Int}}}{\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{{\tt appR}}:~

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{{\bullet^{*}}{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt app}}}{\rangle}{\bullet^{*}}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt ty}}}\;{{\tt Arr}(t_{1},t_{2})}}{\rangle}{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt appL}}}{\rangle}{\bullet^{*}}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt ty}}}\;{t_{1}}}{\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,{\tt unit}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt appR}}}{\rangle}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt ty}}}\;{t_{2}}}{\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{{\tt endAbs}}:~

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{{\bullet^{*}}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt depth}}}\;{d}}{\rangle}{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt abs}}\;{t_{2}}}{\rangle}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt depth}}}\;{d+1}}{\rangle}{\bullet^{*}}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt ty}}}\;{t_{1}}}{\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,{\tt unit}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt endAbs}}}{\rangle}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt depth}}}\;{d}}{\rangle}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt putTy}}}\;{{\tt Arr}(t_{1},t_{2})}}{\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{{\tt var}}:~

\displaystyle i{:}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{}{{\nu}{:}{{\tt int}}\,|\,{\nu\geq 0}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}^{u}}{}{\,\shortrightarrow\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{\underbrace{{\bullet^{*}}{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt abs}}\;{t}}{\rangle}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt depth}}}\;{d}}{\rangle}(\bullet\setminus{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt endAbs}}}{\rangle})^{*}}_{\text{type $t$ at the $d$ level is open}}\underbrace{{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt depth}}}\;{d+i}}{\rangle}}_{\text{current level is $d+i$}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,{\tt unit}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt var}}\;{i}}{\rangle}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt putTy}}}\;{t}}{\rangle}{\bullet^{*}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}

Figure 8
.
u
Hat
specifications of STLC operations.

The types for STLC operations are shown in
Fig. 8
. The type for
\boldsymbol{{\tt const}}
has no constraint on its prefix trace
(
{\bullet^{*}}
), but records a
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt ty}}}
event in
the trace that records the type of the output of the
\boldsymbol{{\tt const}}

expression as
{\tt Int}
. The arguments to a ghost event are either
serialized terms (like
{\tt Int}
) or ghost variables.
Type-checking a trace using ghost events involves solving constraints
over their arguments based on algebraic axiomatizations, examples of
which are given below. The history and future regex of the
\boldsymbol{{\tt appR}}
type in

Fig. 8
characterizes both the syntax and well-typing
constraints of an STLC function application. It requires that (a)
prior to evaluating a function argument (
\boldsymbol{{\tt appL}}
), the type of
the abstraction is
Arr(t
1
,t
2
)
, i.e., the abstraction
being applied is a function type (
{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt ty}}}\;{{\tt Arr}(t_{1},t_{2})}}{\rangle}
); (b) after the argument is evaluated, its type is
determined to be
t
1
(
{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt ty}}}\;{t_{1}}}{\rangle}
); and (c)
the type of the application’s result is
t
2

(
{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt ty}}}\;{t_{2}}}{\rangle}
).

The de Bruijn index constraint is captured via the
\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}depth}}}
operation in the
u
Hat
s of the
\boldsymbol{{\tt endAbs}}
and
\boldsymbol{{\tt var}}
operations. In addition to the well-typing constraint, the
u
Hat
of the
\boldsymbol{{\tt endAbs}}
operation maintains the depth of nested function abstractions using
\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}depth}}}
. Specifically, its history regex obtains the current depth (
{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt depth}}}\;{d}}{\rangle}
), increases it by one at the beginning of the function body, and restores it after the
\boldsymbol{{\tt endAbs}}
event.
On the other hand, a valid index
i
in
\boldsymbol{{\tt var}}
must be derived from an open abstraction at some depth in the prefix trace. The history regex of the
\boldsymbol{{\tt var}}
operation precisely characterizes this constraint: the abstraction at depth
d
must be open (
{\bullet^{*}}{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt abs}}\;{t}}{\rangle}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt depth}}}\;{d}}{\rangle}(\bullet\setminus{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt endAbs}}}{\rangle})^{*}
), and the current depth must be
d+i
.

Appendix C
Operational Semantics

The small-step operational semantics of our core language are shown in

Fig. 9
.

\displaystyle\text{{Handler Semantics }}\ \framebox{$\alpha\vDash\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}({\overline{c}})\Downarrow c$ \hskip 9.24994pt$\mathit{op}(\overline{c})\Downarrow c$}

\displaystyle\text{{Operational Semantics }}\ \framebox{${\alpha}\vDash{e}{\xhookrightarrow{\alpha}\,}{e}$}

StEfOp

\alpha\vDash\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}({\overline{c}})\Downarrow c^{\prime}

{\alpha}\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}\;\overline{c}}{\xhookrightarrow{[\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}({\overline{c}\;c^{\prime}})]}\,}{c^{\prime}}

StOp

\mathit{op}\ \overline{c}\Downarrow c^{\prime}

{\alpha}\vDash{\mathit{op}\ \overline{c}}{\xhookrightarrow{[]}\,}{c^{\prime}}

StAssume

\phi\Downarrow\top

{\alpha}\vDash{{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{assume}}}}}\ {\phi}}{\xhookrightarrow{[]}\,}{()}

StAssert

\phi\Downarrow\top

{\alpha}\vDash{{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{assert}}}}}\ {\phi}}{\xhookrightarrow{[]}\,}{()}

StAssert

\phi\Downarrow\bot

{\alpha}\vDash{{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{assert}}}}}\ {\phi}}{\xhookrightarrow{[]}\,}{{\tt raise\;Error}}

StLetE1

{\alpha}\vDash{e_{1}}{\xhookrightarrow{\alpha^{\prime}}\,}{e_{1}^{\prime}}

{\alpha}\vDash{{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{let}}}}}\ {y}\ {=}\ {e_{1}}\ {\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{in}}}}}\ {e_{2}}}{\xhookrightarrow{\alpha^{\prime}}\,}{{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{let}}}}}\ {y}\ {=}\ {e_{1}^{\prime}}\ {\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{in}}}}}\ {e_{2}}}

StChoice1

{\alpha}\vDash{(e_{1}\oplus e_{2})}{\xhookrightarrow{[]}\,}{(e_{1})}

StLetE2

{\alpha}\vDash{{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{let}}}}}\ {y}\ {=}\ {v}\ {\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{in}}}}}\ {e_{2}}}{\xhookrightarrow{[]}\,}{e_{2}[y\mapsto v]}

StChoice2

{\alpha}\vDash{(e_{1}\oplus e_{2})}{\xhookrightarrow{[]}\,}{(e_{2})}

StAppLam

{\alpha}\vDash{\lambda{x}{:}{s}.{e_{1}}\ v_{x}}{\xhookrightarrow{[]}\,}{e_{1}[x\mapsto v_{x}]}{e_{2}}

StAppFix

{\alpha}\vDash{({\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{fix}}}}}{f}{:}{s}.\lambda{x}{:}{s_{x}}.{e_{1}})\ v_{x}}{\xhookrightarrow{[]}\,}{(\lambda{f}{:}{s}.{e_{1}[x\mapsto v_{x}]})\ ({\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{fix}}}}}{f}{:}{s}.\lambda{x}{:}{s_{x}}.{e_{1}})}

.
Full Operational Semantics

Appendix D
Basic Typing Rules

The basic typing rules of our core language and qualifiers are shown
in
Fig. 10
. We use an auxiliary function

{\tt Ty}
to provide a basic type for the primitives of our language,
e.g., constants, built-in operators, and data constructors.

Basic Typing

\displaystyle\framebox{$\Gamma\vdash_{{\tt s}}e:s$ \hskip 9.24994pt$\Gamma\vdash_{{\tt s}}\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}:s$ \hskip 9.24994pt$\Gamma\vdash_{{\tt s}}\mathit{op}:s$}

BtConst

\Gamma\vdash_{{\tt s}}c:{\tt Ty}(c)

BtVar

\Gamma(x)=s
\Gamma\vdash_{{\tt s}}x:s

BtFun

\Gamma,x{:}b\vdash_{{\tt s}}e:s
\Gamma\vdash_{{\tt s}}\lambda{x}{:}{b}.{e}:b{\,\shortrightarrow\,}s

BtFix

\Gamma,f{:}b{\,\shortrightarrow\,}s,x{:}b\vdash_{{\tt s}}e:s
\Gamma\vdash_{{\tt s}}{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{fix}}}}}{f}{:}{b{\,\shortrightarrow\,}s}.\lambda{x}{:}{b}.{e}:b{\,\shortrightarrow\,}s

BtEfOp

{\tt Ty}(\boldsymbol{{\tt op}})=s
\Gamma\vdash_{{\tt s}}\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}:s

BtPrimOp

{\tt Ty}(\mathit{op})=s
\Gamma\vdash_{{\tt s}}\mathit{op}:s

BtChoice

\Gamma\vdash_{{\tt s}}e_{1}:s\hskip 8.50012pt\Gamma\vdash_{{\tt s}}e_{2}:s
\Gamma\vdash_{{\tt s}}e_{1}\oplus e_{2}:s

BtEfOpApp

\Gamma\vdash_{{\tt s}}\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}:\overline{b_{i}}{\,\shortrightarrow\,}b\hskip 8.50012pt\forall i.\Gamma\vdash_{{\tt s}}c_{i}:b_{i}
\Gamma\vdash_{{\tt s}}\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}\;\overline{c_{i}}:b

BtPrimOpApp

\Gamma\vdash_{{\tt s}}\mathit{op}:\overline{s_{i}}{\,\shortrightarrow\,}s\hskip 8.50012pt\forall i.\Gamma\vdash_{{\tt s}}v_{i}:s_{i}
\Gamma\vdash_{{\tt s}}\mathit{op}\,\overline{v_{i}}:s

BtAssume

\Gamma\vdash_{{\tt s}}\phi:{\tt bool}
\Gamma\vdash_{{\tt s}}{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{assume}}}}}\ {\phi}:{\tt unit}

BtAssert

\Gamma\vdash_{{\tt s}}\phi:{\tt bool}
\Gamma\vdash_{{\tt s}}{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{assert}}}}}\ {\phi}:{\tt unit}

BtLetE

\Gamma\vdash_{{\tt s}}e:s\hskip 8.50012pt\Gamma,y{:}s\vdash_{{\tt s}}e^{\prime}:s
\Gamma\vdash_{{\tt s}}{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{let}}}}}\ {y}\ {=}\ {e}\ {\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{in}}}}}\ {e^{\prime}}:s

BtApp

\Gamma\vdash_{{\tt s}}v_{1}:s{\,\shortrightarrow\,}s^{\prime}\hskip 8.50012pt\Gamma\vdash_{{\tt s}}v_{2}:s
\Gamma\vdash_{{\tt s}}v_{1}\;v_{2}:{\tt unit}

Basic Qualifier Typing

\displaystyle\framebox{$\Gamma\vdash_{{\tt s}}\phi:s$}

BtLitConst

{\tt Ty}(c)=s
\Gamma\vdash_{{\tt s}}c:s

BtLitVar

\Gamma(x)=s
\Gamma\vdash_{{\tt s}}x:s

BtTop

\Gamma\vdash_{{\tt s}}\top:{\tt bool}

BtBot

\Gamma\vdash_{{\tt s}}\bot:{\tt bool}

BtLitOp

{\tt Ty}(\mathit{op})=\overline{s_{i}}{{\,\shortrightarrow\,}}s\hskip 8.50012pt\forall i.\Gamma\vdash_{{\tt s}}l_{i}:s_{i}
\Gamma\vdash_{{\tt s}}\mathit{op}\,\overline{l_{i}}:s

BtNeg

\Gamma\vdash_{{\tt s}}\phi:{\tt bool}
\Gamma\vdash_{{\tt s}}\neg\phi:{\tt bool}

BtAnd

\Gamma\vdash_{{\tt s}}\phi_{1}:{\tt bool}\hskip 8.50012pt\Gamma\vdash_{{\tt s}}\phi_{2}:{\tt bool}
\Gamma\vdash_{{\tt s}}\phi_{1}\land\phi_{2}:{\tt bool}

BtOr

\Gamma\vdash_{{\tt s}}\phi_{1}:{\tt bool}\hskip 8.50012pt\Gamma\vdash_{{\tt s}}\phi_{2}:{\tt bool}
\Gamma\vdash_{{\tt s}}\phi_{1}\lor\phi_{2}:{\tt bool}

BtForall

\Gamma,x{:}b\vdash_{{\tt s}}\phi:{\tt bool}
\Gamma\vdash_{{\tt s}}\forall x{:}b.\phi:{\tt bool}

Type Erasure

\displaystyle\framebox{$\lfloor{t}\rfloor\hskip 9.24994pt\lfloor{\tau}\rfloor\hskip 9.24994pt\lfloor{\Gamma}\rfloor$}

\displaystyle\doteq b\hskip 8.50012pt

\displaystyle\doteq\lfloor{t}\rfloor

\displaystyle\lfloor{\tau_{1}\,\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\sqcap}}\,\tau_{2}}\rfloor

\displaystyle\doteq\emptyset\hskip 8.50012pt

Ghost Event Erasure

\displaystyle\framebox{$\lceil{\alpha}\rceil$}

\displaystyle\lceil{[\;]}\rceil

\displaystyle\doteq[\;]\hskip 8.50012pt

Well-formedness

\displaystyle\framebox{$\Gamma\vdash^{\textbf{WF}}A\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\vdash^{\textbf{WF}}\tau\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\vdash^{\textbf{WF}}t$}

WfEvent

\mathbf{Ty}(\boldsymbol{{\tt op}})=\overline{x_{i}{:}b_{i}}{\,\shortrightarrow\,}b\hskip 8.50012pt\lfloor{\Gamma}\rfloor,\overline{x_{i}{:}b_{i}},\nu{:}b\vdash_{{\tt s}}\phi:{\tt bool}
\Gamma\vdash^{\textbf{WF}}{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}\;{\overline{x_{i}\;\nu}}~|~{\phi}}{\rangle}

WfOr

\Gamma\vdash^{\textbf{WF}}A_{1}\hskip 8.50012pt\Gamma\vdash^{\textbf{WF}}A_{2}
\Gamma\vdash^{\textbf{WF}}A_{1}\lor A_{2}

WfConcat

\Gamma\vdash^{\textbf{WF}}A_{1}\hskip 8.50012pt\Gamma\vdash^{\textbf{WF}}A_{2}
\Gamma\vdash^{\textbf{WF}}A_{1}{\cdot}A_{2}

WfEmpty

\Gamma\vdash^{\textbf{WF}}\emptyset

WfEpsilon

\Gamma\vdash^{\textbf{WF}}\epsilon

WfStar

\Gamma\vdash^{\textbf{WF}}A
\Gamma\vdash^{\textbf{WF}}A^{*}

WfPBase

\lfloor{\Gamma}\rfloor,\nu{:}b\vdash_{{\tt s}}\phi:{\tt bool}
\Gamma\vdash^{\textbf{WF}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{}{{\nu}{:}{b}\,|\,{\phi}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}^{u}}{}

WfPArr

\Gamma\vdash^{\textbf{WF}}t_{x}
\Gamma,x{:}\lfloor{t_{x}}\rfloor\vdash^{\textbf{WF}}t
\Gamma\vdash^{\textbf{WF}}x{:}t_{x}{\,\shortrightarrow\,}t

WfHF

\Gamma,x{:}t\vdash^{\textbf{WF}}H

\Gamma\vdash^{\textbf{WF}}t

\Gamma,x{:}t\vdash^{\textbf{WF}}F

\Gamma\vdash^{\textbf{WF}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{H}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,x{:}t\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{F}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}

WfGEvent

\Gamma,\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}op}}}{:}s\vdash^{\textbf{WF}}t
\Gamma\vdash^{\textbf{WF}}\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}op}}}:s{\,\dashrightarrow\,}t

WfEfArr

\Gamma\vdash^{\textbf{WF}}\tau
\Gamma,x{:}\lfloor{t_{x}}\rfloor\vdash^{\textbf{WF}}\tau
\Gamma\vdash^{\textbf{WF}}x{:}t_{x}{\,\shortrightarrow\,}\tau

WfGArr

\Gamma\vdash^{\textbf{WF}}\tau
\Gamma,x{:}b\vdash^{\textbf{WF}}t
\Gamma\vdash^{\textbf{WF}}x{:}b{\,\dashrightarrow\,}t

WFInter

\Gamma\vdash^{\textbf{WF}}\tau_{1}

\Gamma\vdash^{\textbf{WF}}\tau_{2}

\lfloor{\tau_{1}}\rfloor=\lfloor{\tau_{2}}\rfloor

\Gamma\vdash^{\textbf{WF}}\tau_{1}\,\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\sqcap}}\,\tau_{2}

Full set of well-formedness typing rules.

The full set of rules for our auxiliary typing relations are shown in

Fig. 11
and
Fig. 12
. The full
set of declarative typing rules are shown in

Fig. 13
. We elide the basic typing relation
(
\emptyset\vdash_{{\tt s}}e:s
) in the premises of the rules
in
Fig. 13
; all of these rules assume any
terms they reference have a basic type.

Automata Inclusion

\displaystyle\framebox{$\Gamma\vdash A\subseteq A$}\hskip 9.24994pt

Subtyping

\displaystyle\framebox{$\Gamma\vdash t<:t\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\vdash\tau<:\tau$}

SubAutomata

\forall\sigma_{2}\in\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket.\exists\sigma_{1}\in\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket.\llbracket{\sigma_{2}(A_{2})}\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket{\sigma_{1}(A_{1})}\rrbracket
\Gamma\vdash A_{1}\subseteq A_{2}

SubHF

\Gamma\vdash t_{1}<:t_{2}

\Gamma,x{:}t_{1}\vdash F_{2}\subseteq F_{1}

\Gamma,x{:}t_{1}\vdash H_{1}\subseteq H_{2}

\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{H_{1}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,x{:}t_{1}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{F_{1}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}<:{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{H_{2}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,x{:}t_{2}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{F_{2}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}

SubIntLL

\Gamma\vdash\tau_{1}\,\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\sqcap}}\,\tau_{2}<:\tau_{1}

SubIntLR

\Gamma\vdash\tau_{1}\,\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\sqcap}}\,\tau_{2}<:\tau_{2}

SubIntR

\Gamma\vdash\tau<:\tau_{1}

\Gamma\vdash\tau<:\tau_{2}

\Gamma\vdash\tau<:\tau_{1}\,\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\sqcap}}\,\tau_{2}

SubArr

\Gamma\vdash t_{2}<:t_{1}

\Gamma,x{:}t_{2}\vdash\tau_{1}<:\tau_{2}

\Gamma\vdash x{:}t_{1}{\,\shortrightarrow\,}\tau_{1}<:x{:}t_{2}{\,\shortrightarrow\,}\tau_{2}

SubGRight

\Gamma,x{:}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{}{{\nu}{:}{b}\,|\,{\top}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}^{u}}{}\vdash t_{1}<:t_{2}
\Gamma\vdash t_{1}<:x{:}b{\,\dashrightarrow\,}t_{2}

SubG

\Gamma\vdash^{\textbf{WF}}t[x\mapsto v]

\Gamma\vdash x{:}b{\,\dashrightarrow\,}t<:t[x\mapsto v]

SubGEvent

\Gamma\vdash t_{1}<:t_{2}
\Gamma\vdash\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}op}}}:s{\,\dashrightarrow\,}t_{1}<:\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}op}}}:s{\,\dashrightarrow\,}t_{2}

SubPBase

\forall\sigma_{2}\in\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket.\exists\sigma_{1}\in\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket.\sigma_{1}(\phi_{2})\implies\sigma_{2}(\phi_{2})
\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{}{{\nu}{:}{b}\,|\,{\phi_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}^{u}}{}<:{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{}{{\nu}{:}{b}\,|\,{\phi_{2}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}^{u}}{}

SubPArr

\Gamma\vdash t_{2}<:t_{1}

\Gamma,x{:}t_{2}\vdash\tau_{1}<:\tau_{2}

\Gamma\vdash x{:}t_{1}{\,\shortrightarrow\,}\tau_{1}<:x{:}t_{2}{\,\shortrightarrow\,}\tau_{2}

Auxiliary Typing

Typing

TRet

\Gamma\vdash v:t

\Gamma\vdash v:{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{H}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,t\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}

TOpCtx

\Delta(\boldsymbol{{\tt op}})=t

\Gamma\vdash\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}:t

TEffOp

\Gamma\vdash\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}:\overline{x_{i}{:}t_{i}}{\,\shortrightarrow\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{H}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,t\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}\;{\overline{x_{i}}}}{\rangle}{\cdot}F}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}

\forall i.\;\Gamma\vdash v_{i}:t_{i}

\Gamma\vdash\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}{\;\overline{v_{i}}}:{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{H}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,t\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}\;{\overline{v_{i}}}}{\rangle}{\cdot}F}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}

TChoice

\Gamma\vdash e_{1}:\tau_{1}

\Gamma\vdash e_{2}:\tau_{2}

\Gamma\vdash e_{1}\oplus e_{2}:\tau_{1}\,\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\sqcap}}\,\tau_{2}

TLet

\Gamma\vdash e_{1}:{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{H}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,x{:}t_{x}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{F_{1}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}

\Gamma,x{:}t_{x}\vdash e_{2}:{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{H{\cdot}F_{1}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,t\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{F_{2}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}

\Gamma\vdash{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{let}}}}}\ {x}\ {=}\ {e_{1}}\ {\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{in}}}}}\ {e_{2}}:{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{H}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,t\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{F_{1}{\cdot}F_{2}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}

TFun

\Gamma,x{:}t_{x}\vdash e:\tau

\Gamma\vdash\lambda{x}.{e}:x{:}t_{x}{\,\shortrightarrow\,}\tau

TFixBase

\Gamma\vdash{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{fix}}}}}{f}.\lambda{x}.{e}:\overline{x{:}s}{\,\shortrightarrow\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{H}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}{\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{}{{\nu}{:}{b}\,|\,{\bot}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}^{u}}{}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{[}}}{}{\emptyset}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{]}}}{}

TFixInd

\Gamma\vdash{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{fix}}}}}{f}.\lambda{x}.{e}:t

\Gamma,f{:}t\vdash\lambda{x}.{e}:t^{\prime}

\Gamma\vdash{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{fix}}}}}{f}.\lambda{x}.{e}:t^{\prime}

TConst

\lfloor{\Gamma}\rfloor\vdash_{{\tt s}}c:b

\Gamma\vdash c:{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{}{{\nu}{:}{b}\,|\,{\nu=c}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}^{u}}{}

TVar

\lfloor{\Gamma}\rfloor\vdash_{{\tt s}}x:b

\Gamma\vdash x:{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{}{{\nu}{:}{b}\,|\,{\nu=x}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}^{u}}{}

TAppEff

\Gamma v_{1}:x{:}t_{2}{\,\shortrightarrow\,}\tau

\Gamma\vdash v_{2}:t_{2}

\Gamma\vdash v_{1}v_{2}:\tau[x\mapsto v_{2}]

TOpApp

\Gamma\vdash\mathit{op}:\overline{y{:}t_{i}}{\,\shortrightarrow\,}t

\forall i.\Gamma\vdash v_{i}:t_{i}

\Gamma\vdash\mathit{op}\ \overline{v}:t

TPureOp

\Delta(\mathit{op})=t

\Gamma\vdash\mathit{op}:t

TPureSub

\Gamma\vdash v:t

\Gamma\vdash t<:t^{\prime}

\Gamma\vdash v:t^{\prime}

TSub

\Gamma\vdash e:\tau

\Gamma\vdash\tau<:\tau^{\prime}

\Gamma\vdash e:\tau^{\prime}

TOpHis

Appendix G
Auxiliary Functions for Synthesis

This section describes three auxiliary functions used for generator
synthesis. The first of these,
\mathbf{Norm}
, converts a SRE formula
into a set of abstract traces. The second,
\mathbf{TermDerive}
, generates test generators from a set of abstract traces. The third,

\mathbf{termTrace}
, generates a straightline program from a single refined abstract trace.

Procedure

\mathbf{Norm}(A):=

1

A\leftarrow{\tt Minimize}(A)
;

2

match

A
:

3

case

\emptyset

do

return

\emptyset
;

4

case

\epsilon

do

return

\{\epsilon\}
;

5

{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}~|~{\phi}}{\rangle}

do

return

\{{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}~|~{\phi}}{\rangle}^{\bot}\}
;

6

case

A_{1}{\cdot}A_{2}

do

return

\{\pi_{1}{\cdot}\pi_{2}~|~\pi_{1}\in\mathbf{Norm}(A_{1})\land\pi_{2}\in\mathbf{Norm}(A_{2})\}
;

7

case

A_{1}\lor A_{2}

do

return

\mathbf{Norm}(A_{1})\cup\mathbf{Norm}(A_{2})
;

8

case

A^{*}

do

\mathbf{Norm}(A)

Algorithm 3

Abstract Trace Normalization

Normalization

The SRE normalization function
\mathbf{Norm}{}
, shown in

Algorithm 3
, first perform a standard symbolic automaton minimization step to remove intersections and complements, and then recursively translates an input SRE into a set of
abstract traces. It divides
A\lor A^{\prime}
into multiple abstract
traces, and leaves the Kleene star over multiple singleton events
unchanged.

Lemma G.1.

[Normalization is sound]
The normalized result has the same denotation as the input SRE, that
is, for all SRE
A
and set of traces
\{\overline{\pi}\}
,
\llbracket{A}\rrbracket=\bigcup_{i}\llbracket{\pi_{i}}\rrbracket

Procedure

\mathbf{TermDerive}(C):=

1

E\leftarrow\emptyset
;

2

foreach

(\Gamma,\pi)\in C

do

3

e_{1}\leftarrow\mathbf{DeriveTrace}(\Gamma,\pi)
;

4

e_{2}\leftarrow\mathbf{SynRecursion}(\Gamma,\pi,{\tt UnrollingBound})
;

5

E\leftarrow E\cup\{e_{1},e_{2}\}
;

6

return

\bigoplus_{e\in E}e
;

7

Procedure

\mathbf{DeriveTrace}(\Gamma,\pi):=

8

match

\Gamma
:

9

case

[]

do

return

\mathbf{DeriveTraceAux}(\pi)
;

10

case

x{:}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@lbrace}}}{}{{\nu}{:}{b}\,|\,{\phi}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.4883,0.0313}\mbox{\rm{\lx@text@rbrace}}^{u}}{}\;{::}\;\Gamma^{\prime}

do

11

return

{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{let}}}}}\ {\overline{x}}={\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{assume}}}}}\ {\phi[\nu\mapsto x]}\ {\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{in}}}}}\ {\mathbf{DeriveTrace}(\Gamma^{\prime},\pi)}
;

12

\mathbf{DeriveTraceAux}(\Pi):=

\Pi
:

\epsilon

;

return

\mathbf{DeriveTraceAux}(\pi^{\prime})

{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt op}}}\;{\overline{x}}~|~{\phi}}{\rangle}{\cdot}\pi^{\prime}

17

19

{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{let}}}}}\ {\overline{x^{\prime}}}={\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{assume}}}}}\ {\phi_{1}}\ {\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{in}}}}}\ {e}

Algorithm 4

Term Derivation

Term Derivation

The term derivation function

\mathbf{TermDerive}
is shown in
Algorithm 4
. It first
converts the input type context into
{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{assume}}}}}
statements
that use the type qualifiers attached to each variable (line 5), and
then derives the abstract trace with the help of the

\mathbf{DeriveTrace}
, which recursively transforms the abstract trace
into a straightline generator program. Any Kleene stars are dropped
(line 9), ghost events are converted into
{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{assume}}}}}

expressions (line 14), and concrete events are converted into
effectful operations (line 16-21). Note that the arguments of the
effectful operation are provided by
{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{assume}}}}}
and the values
they return are always checked by
{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{assert}}}}}
(line 21).

Procedure

\mathbf{SynRecursion}(\Gamma,\Pi,{\tt UnrollingBound}):=

1

(T,\{\pi_{i}\})\leftarrow\mathbf{MatchTemplate}(\Pi)
;

2

if

\forall i.0\leq i<{\tt UnrollingBound}.\exists\tau.\Gamma\vdash{\tt Unroll}(T,\{\pi_{i}\},i):\tau

then

3

e_{i}\leftarrow\mathbf{DeriveTrace}(\pi_{i})
;

4

return

T(\overline{e_{i}})

Algorithm 5

Synthesis of Recursive Programs

Recursion Synthesis

Kleene stars (
\Pi^{*}
) in abstract
traces are interpreted as recursion points. The synthesis algorithm
can synthesize a recursive program by expanding the Kleene star using
recursion. As shown in
Algorithm 5
, we employ a
template-based strategy, in which the algorithm matches the given
abstract trace with a template
T
, together with a set of abstract
traces
\{\pi_{i}\}
that are matched from the orginal trace. As
dictated by
TFixInd
, the algorithm unrolls the recursive body
up to a bounded depth and invokes refinement loop (lines 3 in

Algorithm 5
) to ensure that the unrolled abstract
traces are well-typed. Finally, the synthesized program is
instantiated by the template with derived programs from these abstract
traces.

Example G.2 (Recursion Template).

A representative template is a non-deterministic loop, whose form is

T\doteq{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{let}}}}}\ {f}\ {=}\ {{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{fix}}}}}\ f.\lambda().\ ()\oplus\boxed{e_{1}};f\;();\boxed{e_{2}}}\ {\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.0313,0.0039,0.457}{in}}}}}\ {\boxed{e_{3}};f\;();\boxed{e_{4}}}
, allowing

\Pi^{*}
in the abstract trace to be matched. For instance, consider
the abstract trace
\pi_{\tt stack}
shown in
Example
4.2
:

\displaystyle\underbrace{\Pi_{\tt noI}^{*}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt pushI}}}\;{m\;y}}{\rangle}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt popI}}}\;{m\;y}}{\rangle}\Pi_{\tt noI}^{*}}_{e_{3}}\;\;\underbrace{{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt push}}\;{x}}{\rangle}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt pushI}}}\;{n\;x}}{\rangle}}_{e_{1}}\;\;\underbrace{\Pi_{\tt noI}^{*}}_{\text{recursion}}\;\;\underbrace{{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}\;{x}}{\rangle}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt popI}}}\;{n\;x}}{\rangle}}_{e_{2}}\;\;\underbrace{\Pi_{\tt noI}^{*}}_{e_{4}}

Here,
\pi_{\tt stack}
is divided into five parts, corresponding
to the four placeholders and the self-recursive call (i.e.,
f\;()
)
in the template. The synthesizer then re-refines the abstract trace for
i
-fold
recursion unrolling after erasing all qualifiers from the abstract
trace:

(0-fold unrolling)

\displaystyle\Pi_{\tt anyA}^{*}{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}pushI}}}}{\rangle}{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}popI}}}}{\rangle}

\displaystyle\;\underline{\epsilon}\;\Pi_{\tt anyA}^{*}

(1-fold unrolling)

\displaystyle\Pi_{\tt anyA}^{*}{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}pushI}}}}{\rangle}{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}popI}}}}{\rangle}\Pi_{\tt anyA}^{*}\;\underline{{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}pushI}}}}{\rangle}{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt push}}}{\rangle}}

\displaystyle\underline{{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}popI}}}}{\rangle}{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}}{\rangle}}\;\Pi_{\tt anyA}^{*}

(2-fold unrolling)

\displaystyle\Pi_{\tt anyA}^{*}{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}pushI}}}}{\rangle}{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}popI}}}}{\rangle}\Pi_{\tt anyA}^{*}\;\underline{{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}pushI}}}}{\rangle}{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt push}}}{\rangle}{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}pushI}}}}{\rangle}{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt push}}}{\rangle}}

\displaystyle\underline{{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}popI}}}}{\rangle}{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}}{\rangle}{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}popI}}}}{\rangle}{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}}{\rangle}}\;\Pi_{\tt anyA}^{*}

This refinement ensures that multiple unfoldings remain well-typed,
which disallows an incorrect recursion template instantiation, as
shown below:

\displaystyle\underbrace{\Pi_{\tt noI}^{*}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt pushI}}}\;{m\;y}}{\rangle}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt popI}}}\;{m\;y}}{\rangle}\Pi_{\tt noI}^{*}{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt push}}\;{x}}{\rangle}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt pushI}}}\;{n\;x}}{\rangle}}_{e_{3}}\;\;\underbrace{\Pi_{\tt noI}^{*}}_{e_{1}}\;\;\underbrace{\Pi_{\tt noI}^{*}}_{\text{recursion}}\;\;\underbrace{{\langle}{\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}\;{x}}{\rangle}{\langle}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\boldsymbol{{\tt popI}}}\;{n\;x}}{\rangle}}_{e_{2}}\;\;\underbrace{\Pi_{\tt noI}^{*}}_{e_{4}}

This instantiation will repeatedly perform
\boldsymbol{{\tt pop}}

events without any
\boldsymbol{{\tt push}}
events; its two-fold recursion
unrolling will therefore be rejected. The final recursive program
is assembled by composing synthesized programs from these traces to
populate the template placeholders, thereby yielding the following
program:

⬇

1

let

=

()

\

?

let

(
x
:

int
)

=

(*

(

:

)
